Predicted identities were separated into cell-type and ERS-state components, and stability was assessed independently for both. Cell-type stability was quantified as the fraction of parameter sets in which each cell retained its baseline cell-type assignment, whereas ERS stability was calculated as the consistency of ERS+ and ERS− assignment in the baseline cell type across parameter sets. Global agreement between parameter sets was further quantified using pairwise adjusted rand index (ARI) comparisons across all transferred labels. Furthermore, the proportion of ERS+ cells in each cell type, the fraction of cells retaining their baseline cell type assignment and the frequency of ERS state switching across parameter sets were calculated independently for each external dataset.

The fragment files generated with Cell Ranger ATAC (v.1.2) and mapped to TAIR10/Araport11 reference were obtained from a previous study16, imported to snapatac2 (v.2.6.4) and paired-insertions calculated77 in 500 bp bins78. The cells were initially defined as barcodes associated with more than 3,500 and fewer than 50,000 fragments per barcode and with a TSS enrichment score higher than 2.5. Low-quality cells and outliers were also identified per-sample basis using the 3× median absolute deviation threshold and further removed from analysis79. Following peak calling using macs3 (ref. 80), peak and gene activity matrices were calculated using the paired-insertion counting strategy. For ATAC–seq data integration into our scRNA-seq dataset, the peak matrices were imported to the Signac package81 and the dimensions of ATAC–seq were reduced using latent semantic indexing. ATAC–seq nuclei were assigned identities using our reference atlas and the FindTransferAnchors function with the parameters reduction = ‘cca’ and dims = 1:30, followed by TransferData with weight.reduction = obj[[“lsi”]] and dims = 2:30. The TransferData function and previously computed anchors were also used to generate an ‘imputed’ scRNA-seq matrix for the ATAC nuclei. Using this imputed matrix, the snATAC–seq and scRNA-seq reference atlases were merged into a single object, processed with ScaleData and RunPCA, and the first 30 principal components were used to produce a UMAP co-embedding of the two datasets.

To perform differential expression analysis of our co-expression modules, a Seurat object was created with all conditions to compare, and the data were scaled using ScaleData (Seurat v.5.0.0). The modules were projected into the new dataset using ProjectModules (hdWGCNA v.0.4.1). A module score was then calculated using ModuleExprScore. The differential module scores were determined using the FindDMEs function with features = “ModuleScores” and test.use = “wilcox”. The results were plotted using PlotDMEsLollipop.

All changes in cell composition were tested with sccomp (v.1.99.18) in R82. Specifically, when evaluating changes in ERS+ (and consequently ERS−) proportions, the Seurat object was subsetted to retain only cells that had reached the developmental stage at which they could be assigned to either state to avoid taking into account sampling effects that might affect the total number of mature cells captured. This process excluded the root cap, ‘early’ identities and the non-hair cells, for which the trajectories could determine the point of branching. The test was then run on the subsetted data using sccomp_estimate and sccomp_test, and results were plotted with sccomp_boxplot or with plot in the case of the water-deficit experiment for which the covariate is continuous.

Seedlings were grown vertically on full (1×) or half (0.5×) MS plates, as for scRNA-seq experiments. Pictures of the roots were taken at 6 d.a.g., using a stereomicroscope (n = 16 for full MS, n = 23 for half MS, from two different plates each). A portion corresponding to approximately 2.5 mm from the tip was selected for each image of the root, and root hairs were manually counted using the Cell Counter plugin in Fiji. A significant difference in root hair density between the two conditions was determined using a Wilcoxon rank-sum test (wilcox.test function) in R.

Note that the seedlings used for this quantification were in the top row of the plate (seeds were sown in two rows for scRNA-seq experiments). Roots of seedlings grown in the bottom row tended to have fewer root hairs (in both conditions).

A new Seurat object was created using the Col-0 cells that were grown on full MS (which is all replicates except the three half MS replicates), as well as the cells of the C24, Cvi-0, Ler and Ws-2 atlases. The expression data were then pseudobulked per genotype, cell identity and batch using the AggregateExpression in Seurat (v.5.0.0). For each possible inter-accession replicate pair, a differential expression test was performed on the pseudobulk data using FindMarkers with DESeq2. Genes were considered differentially expressed if the adjusted P < 0.05 and the absolute log2 fold-change was >0.5. For a gene to be called an accession-specific DEG, it had to be systematically differentially expressed in a given accession compared with the other four, but not be differentially expressed in any pairwise comparison between the remaining four accessions. The proportion of ecotype-specific DEGs per identity or per module were plotted using pheatmap.

Module scores for hdWGCNA modules 20 and 21 were calculated using AddModuleScore in Seurat (v.5.0.0). Analyses were restricted to cells classified as ERS+ or ERS− from the 100% and 20% water conditions. Total UMI counts were log10-transformed after addition of a pseudocount. Module scores were analysed using linear mixed-effects models implemented in the lme4 (v.1.1.36) and lmerTest (v.3.2.1) R packages, with water condition as a fixed effect, log10 UMI count modelled using a natural spline with four degrees of freedom (splines v.4.3.1 package) and biological replicate included as a random intercept. For visualization, cells were divided into ten equal-frequency bins according to log10 UMI count, and mean module scores ± s.e.m. were calculated in each bin. Binning was used for visualization only; all statistical inference was performed on the unbinned cell-level data. The association between water condition and ERS identity was assessed using binomial generalized linear mixed-effects models (glmer; lme4) with biological replicate included as a random intercept. An unadjusted model included water condition alone, whereas the adjusted model also included log10 UMI count as a fixed-effect covariate. Odds ratios and 95% confidence intervals for the effect of water condition were obtained using broom.mixed (v.0.2.9.6).

To compare roots grown under different conditions using whole-mount HCR, all roots were grown, processed and imaged simultaneously using identical confocal settings.

First, a mask defining the cytoplasmic area of the root was created in Fiji. A threshold-based mask of the whole root was generated from the calcofluor channel using Image > Adjust > Threshold, followed by Process > Binary > Close and Process > Binary > Fill Holes. A second threshold-based mask was created from the calcofluor channel to define cell walls. Subtracting the cell wall mask from the whole-root mask (Process > Math > Subtract) produced a mask containing only the cytoplasmic regions. This mask was then multiplied by a mask selecting the region from the elongation zone upwards, corresponding to the area where ERS expression begins in single-cell data.

The resulting cytoplasmic mask was multiplied by the channel containing the HCR signal to retain only signal in cells from the elongation zone upwards. A separate threshold-based mask was created from the HCR signal channel to identify areas above a defined signal threshold. Multiplying this mask by the cytoplasmic mask ensured that only signal inside cells from the elongation zone upwards was considered.

Custom Fiji macros were used to automate measurements across z-stacks. One macro measured the surface area covered by a mask in each slice, which enabled calculation of the total cytoplasmic area for normalization and the area of positive signal. Another macro measured the sum of all pixel intensities per slice, which was applied to the cytoplasmic signal image.

Differences in signal between conditions were assessed using the Wilcoxon rank-sum test.

Single-cell segmentation was performed in 3D using the cell-wall staining channel. Individual cells were first manually seeded by placing markers in each cell lumen and storing these positions as regions of interest. A binary marker image was then generated from the seed positions. The cell wall channel was processed using the MorphoLibJ83 Morphological Filters (3D) function with the Gradient filter to enhance cell boundaries. The processed cell-wall image and binary marker image were subsequently used for marker-controlled watershed segmentation using MorphoLibJ. Segmentation outputs were manually curated to correct improper segmentations caused by discontinuities or holes in cell-wall staining, either by removing incorrectly segmented cells or merging oversegmented regions. Before fluorescence quantification, signal channels were background-corrected to minimize the contribution of imaging noise and diffuse background fluorescence. In brief, pixel intensities were measured in a region outside the root tissue, and the 99th percentile background intensity value was subtracted from all pixels in the image using the Subtract function in Fiji. The resulting filtered fluorescence images were combined with the curated segmentation masks to quantify volumetric single-cell mean fluorescence intensity using the Intensity Measurements 2D/3D function in MorphoLibJ. Measured fluorescence values were then mapped back onto segmented cells using the Assign Measure to Labels function in MorphoLibJ to generate colour-coded single-cell fluorescence representations.

For the water-deficit HCR experiments, segmentation was restricted to epidermal and cortical cells. Fluorescence quantification was performed on the original, non-background-subtracted images so that identical image processing was applied across control and water-deficit samples, which enabled direct comparison of fluorescence intensities between conditions. Mean fluorescence intensity was measured for each segmented cell. To account for batch-to-batch differences in HCR signal intensity, the mean fluorescence intensity for each segmented cell was normalized to the mean per-cell fluorescence of the corresponding 100% water control sample processed in parallel. Empirical cumulative distribution functions were then generated to compare the distributions of normalized single-cell fluorescence intensities between conditions.

To test whether mutually exclusive marker expression defined two separable cell populations, Gaussian mixture modelling was applied independently to fluorescence quantification data and scRNA-seq expression data using the R package mclust84 (v.6.0.0). For fluorescence imaging data, mean single-cell fluorescence intensities for AT3G01720 and AT5G56550 were extracted from segmented cells following background correction and volumetric quantification. A marker ratio was calculated for each cell as AT3G01720/(AT3G01720 + AT5G56550), and Gaussian mixture modelling was performed on the resulting distribution using Mclust. Cells were assigned to the inferred populations based on maximum posterior probability. The fitted Gaussian components and their relative proportions were visualized over the observed ratio distribution. For scRNA-seq data, normalized expression values for AT3G01720 and AT5G56550 were extracted from hair cells in the reference atlas. Cells with zero combined expression for both markers were excluded from the analysis. A marker score was then calculated as the difference between AT3G01720 and AT5G56550 expression values, and Gaussian mixture modelling was applied to the score distribution using Mclust. Fitted Gaussian distributions were visualized together with the observed score distribution to assess the presence of two transcriptionally distinct populations.

a, UMAP plots showing the expression of previously characterised marker genes associated with distinct stages and subdomains of root cap development, including CDF4 (differentiating columella), FEZ (early root cap), RCPG (root cap tip), and BFN1 (lateral programmed cell death zone), validating the biological interpretation of the inferred root cap trajectories based on their known in vivo expression patterns during root cap development13,14,15. b–d, Validation of pseudotime assignments by comparison of in silico expression patterns and whole-mount HCR in intact roots. In the endodermis (b), AT2G14900, predicted to be expressed early according to the trajectory analysis, is detected closer to the meristem, whereas AT2G36100, a later marker, is detected further away, consistent with the inferred developmental progression. Similarly, in the xylem pole pericycle (c), AT2G18800 (early marker) is detected nearer to the meristem and AT3G47510 (late marker) further away. In the epidermis (d), AT1G66470 (early marker) and AT4G40090 (late marker) also show concordant expression patterns in silico and in vivo. Dotted lines indicate the earliest developmental stage at which expression of each marker was detected. Multiple optical sections are shown when a complete longitudinal section of the root could not be obtained at once due to minor root curvature during mounting. Representative images are shown; results were reproduced in at least two independent experiments with similar results. Scale bar = 50 μm. e, UMAP plot summarising the diffusion pseudotime assignment for each lineage in the reference atlas.

a, Scatter plots and regression lines showing the distribution of expression scores from our Col-0 scRNA-seq atlas for the top 50 genes associated with the meristematic, elongation, and maturation zones defined by Shahan et al.8, plotted as a function of diffusion pseudotime for each cell type. Successive peaks of meristematic, elongation, and maturation scores are observed along the pseudotime axis, consistent with the expected developmental progression and validating the inferred trajectories. b–e, Monocle trajectory analysis. b, UMAP plot showing the partitions defined by Monocle, for which independent trajectory analyses were computed. Endodermis and cortex, xylem and phloem pole pericycle, and procambium and phloem are grouped within the same partition. c, UMAP plot showing the pseudotime ordering obtained with Monocle. Arrows indicate shifts in inferred t = 0 placement relative to the reference atlas. d, UMAP plot showing the resulting Monocle trajectories, recovering the same two branches identified across cell types by the PAGA trajectory analysis. e, Scatter plots showing Monocle pseudotime (scaled from 0 to 1) and diffusion pseudotime assignment per cell, by cell type, demonstrating strong concordance between the two methods. Spearman correlation coefficients and cell numbers are indicated in the upper left of each panel. f–g, RNA velocity analysis using scVelo. f, UMAP plot showing the latent time assignment obtained with scVelo. Arrows indicate shifts in inferred t = 0 placement relative to the reference atlas. g, Scatter plots showing scVelo latent time and diffusion pseudotime assignment per cell, by cell type. Spearman correlation coefficients and cell numbers are indicated in the upper left of each panel. Lower correlations in some lineages are primarily explained by slight differences in inferred t = 0 placement between the velocity analysis and the other trajectory inference methods.

a–c, Cell cycle analysis. a, UMAP plot showing the predicted cell cycle phase of cells in the reference atlas. b, Bar plot showing the proportion of cells in each predicted cell cycle phase per cluster. c, UMAP plot of the reference atlas after regression of cell cycle effects, showing that the bifurcating trajectories are maintained. d, UMAP plot showing cells in the reference atlas coloured by experimental batch. e, Box plots showing the distribution of the log2 fold-change in expression following protoplast isolation for genes belonging to the co-expression modules identified in Fig. 2b,c (n = 8 biologically independent samples per condition over 2 independent experiments). Branch 1-specific modules 20 and 21 are not preferentially up- or down-regulated during protoplast isolation. Positive fold-change values indicate higher transcript abundance in protoplasts. f–h, Analysis of roots grown penetrating vertically through the agar rather than along the agar surface. f, Diagram illustrating the two experimental growth conditions. g, UMAP plot showing cell identity assignments for roots grown penetrating through agar using the reference atlas. The bifurcating trajectories are also detected in this dataset. h, Projection of cells from roots penetrating through agar onto the reference atlas UMAP embedding, showing that cells distribute across all branches similarly to the reference atlas. i,j, Distribution of the percentage of mitochondrial (i) and chloroplast (j) transcripts in cells assigned to ‘branch 1’ (ERS+), ‘branch 2’ (ERS−), or without branch assignment (root cap and early pre-branching cells) (n = 87,056 cells from the reference atlas across 9 independent experiments). Only a few dozen cells in the >87,000-cell reference atlas exceed 5%, a commonly used threshold for low-quality cells. ‘Branch 2’ cells, despite their lower UMI content, show mitochondrial and chloroplast transcript proportions comparable to root cap and early cells, suggesting they are unlikely to represent damaged or low-quality cells. For e, i and j, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

a–c, Analysis of gene expression differences between ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) and ‘branch 2’ (ERS−) in the xylem pole pericycle. a, Scatter plot showing the average expression in ‘branch 1’ versus ‘branch 2’ cells for genes from two categories of co-expression modules: ‘branch 1’-specific modules (Modules 20 and 21; n = 935 genes) and modules expressed in both branches (Modules 13 and 14; n = 329 genes). The dotted line indicates the identity line (x = y), and the solid black line indicates the linear regression fitted using genes expressed in both branches. Dashed vertical lines indicate the overlapping range of ‘branch 1’ expression between the two gene categories, defined from the common 10th to 90th percentile range and used for the controlled analysis in panel c. b, Distribution of average expression in ‘branch 1’ cells for the two gene categories (n = 935 genes for Modules 20 and 21; n = 329 genes for Modules 13 and 14). Dashed horizontal lines indicate the same overlapping expression range shown in panel a. Although genes expressed in both branches extend further into the upper tail of the distribution, both gene categories include genes within the same lower expression range. c, Residual ‘branch 2’ expression, calculated as the observed ‘branch 2’ expression minus the expression expected from the regression fitted in panel a. Residuals are shown for genes within the overlapping ‘branch 1’ expression range indicated in panels a and b (n = 747 genes for Modules 20 and 21; n = 209 genes for Modules 13 and 14). ‘Branch 1’-specific genes show significantly lower residual ‘branch 2’ expression than genes expressed in both branches (two-sided Wilcoxon rank-sum test, p-value < 0.0001), indicating that their absence from ‘branch 2’ cannot be explained solely by lower expression or reduced transcript detection in cells with lower UMI content. d–g, UMI downsampling analysis. d,e, Distribution of UMI content per cell identity in the reference atlas before (d) and after (e) UMI downsampling (n = 87,056 cells from the reference atlas across 9 independent experiments). UMI content was downsampled in ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) cells to span the same range as their ‘branch 2’ (ERS−) counterparts. f,g, UMAP plots showing cell identities (f) and meristematic score used for pseudotime assignment (g) after three independent UMI downsampling computations. Bifurcating trajectories are detected in all three downsampling replicates, with cells showing the highest meristematic score per lineage consistently positioned between branches, as in the reference atlas. For b–e, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

Box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers). i, Pairwise Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) comparisons between all parameter combinations for transferred labels, showing strong global agreement across projections. j, Fraction of cells retaining their baseline cell type assignment across parameter combinations. Dashed line indicates the baseline parameter set used throughout the study (50 principal components, k.anchor = 5; condition 3). k, Proportion of cells retaining the same branch (ERS) state, switching branch (ERS) state, or losing branch (ERS) assignment relative to the baseline parameter set. The vast majority of cells retained both their cell type identity and branch (ERS) state assignment across parameter combinations.

a, Longitudinal optical sections of roots used to visualise the expression of the four branch 1-specific genes shown in Fig. 3a. Yellow rectangles mark the portions depicted in Fig. 3a. Scale bar = 50 μm. b, Expression of the branch 1-specific gene AT4G24060 (VDOF1). Left, UMAP plot showing the branch 1-specific expression of VDOF1 in silico. Right, longitudinal optical section showing VDOF1 expression by whole-mount HCR. Cells enriched (+) or not (−) for VDOF1 transcripts in the endodermis (en.) or pericycle (pe.) are indicated by arrows. Scale bar = 50 μm. c, Validation that absence of branch 1 marker signal in negative cells is not due to failed HCR detection. Top, UMAP plots showing the expression patterns of the ‘branch 1’-associated marker AT2G41660 (MIZ1) and endodermal marker AT2G14900 in the reference atlas. AT2G41660 is enriched specifically in branch 1 endodermal cells, whereas AT2G14900 is expressed throughout the endodermis independently of branch state. Bottom, representative whole-mount HCR images showing simultaneous detection of AT2G41660 (magenta) and AT2G14900 (cyan) in Arabidopsis root endodermal cells. While AT2G41660 signal is restricted to a subset of endodermal cells, consistent with branch 1-associated expression, AT2G14900 is robustly detected in both AT2G41660-positive and AT2G41660-negative cells. Arrows indicate examples of endodermal cells lacking detectable AT2G41660 signal but retaining clear AT2G14900 expression. These results demonstrate that the absence of branch 1 marker signal reflects genuine biological variation rather than differences in probe performance or hybridisation efficiency. Scale bar = 50 μm. The HCR experiments shown in a–c were independently repeated at least twice with similar results.

a, Box plots showing the distribution of ERS+ and ERS− cell proportions per branching cell type in the reference Col-0 atlas and in the two ABA-related mutants, aba2-3 and pyl11458 (Col-0, n = 6 biologically independent experiments; aba2-3 and pyl11458, n = 3 biologically independent experiments each). Each dot represents an individual replicate. Tests showing a significant difference in cell state proportion compared to Col-0 are highlighted in pink (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). b–d, Analysis of the scr-3 mutant. b, UMAP plots showing our reference atlas and the projection of cells from scr-3 replicates into the atlas UMAP embedding, with cells coloured by their assigned identity based on our atlas. Consistent with previous reports8, the single ground tissue layer initially exhibits a cortex-like identity before progressively adopting endodermal identity. c, scr-3 cells shown in their atlas UMAP embedding. d, Box plots showing the distribution of cell types in the reference Col-0 atlas and in the scr-3 mutant (Col-0, n = 6 biologically independent experiments; scr-3, n = 3 biologically independent experiments). Each dot represents an individual replicate. Tests showing a significant difference in cell type proportion compared to Col-0 are highlighted in pink (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). Although columella and pericycle identities are strongly depleted, the corresponding cell layers remain physically present. Conversely, adjacent cell identities are enriched, raising the possibility that these cells have adopted neighbouring identities.

a,b, Root phenotypes and ERS proportions in full MS versus half MS conditions. a, Top, representative images of roots grown vertically on full MS or half MS plates, 6 days after germination. Scale bar = 1 mm. Bottom, box plot showing the distribution of the number of root hairs in the first 2.5 mm of roots grown on full MS or half MS (full MS, n = 16 roots; half MS, n = 23 roots; two plates per condition). Asterisks indicate p-value < 0.001 (two-sided Wilcoxon rank-sum test). b, Box plots showing the distribution of ERS+ and ERS− cell proportions per branching cell type in three replicates grown simultaneously on either full MS or half MS medium (n = 3 biologically independent experiments per condition). Tests showing a significant difference in cell state proportion between conditions are highlighted in pink (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). c,d, Cell type and ERS state distributions under the “hard agar” drought model; lines and bands indicate estimates and 95% credible intervals (sccomp). c, Scatter plots with regression lines showing the distribution of root cap cells, early developmental-stage cells prior to ERS+/− branching (‘epidermis early’, ‘hair cells early’, and ‘protoxylem early’), and cells that have reached the ERS+/− stage across 100%, 50% and 20% H2O. Significant changes are highlighted by rectangles (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). d, ERS+ and ERS− cell proportions per branching cell type across the same conditions. Significant changes are highlighted by rectangles (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). e, UMI counts in ERS−, ERS+ and non-ERS cells under 100% H2O (n = 25,964 cells, 2 independent experiments) and 20% H2O (n = 12,882 cells, 2 independent experiments). Violin plots show cell-level distributions. FC, fold change. f, Mean ERS module 20/21 scores across log10 UMI bins; points show means and error bars indicate s.e.m. Linear mixed-effects models tested differences between water conditions while adjusting for log10 UMI counts (p-value < 1 × 10−4, module 20; p-value = 0.016, module 21; 100% H2O, n = 14,771 cells; 20% H2O, n = 4,962 cells; 2 independent experiments per condition; two-sided tests). g, Odds ratios (OR) for ERS+ classification from binomial generalized linear mixed-effects models before and after log10 UMI adjustment. Points indicate OR estimates and horizontal lines indicate 95% confidence intervals (CI): OR = 3.2 (95% CI, 2.2–4.8; p-value < 1 × 10−4) unadjusted and OR = 2.0 (95% CI, 1.4–3.1; p-value = 0.00061) after adjustment (same n as f; two-sided tests). h, Proportion of cells assigned as ERS+ plotted against the proportion of cells filtered during preprocessing for each library. No correlation is observed between these two metrics, indicating that measured changes in ERS+ proportions are not driven by differential filtering of low-UMI ERS− cells. i, Increased ERS marker expression under drought conditions is not explained by reduced root size. Left, representative whole-mount HCR images showing expression of the ERS marker AT3G04010 in control roots at 3 and 6 days after germination (dag). Despite the substantially smaller size of 3 dag roots, fluorescence intensity at the root tip remains comparable between stages. Right, quantification of AT3G04010 HCR signal intensity normalised by root area in 3 dag and 6 dag control roots (n = 2 independent biological samples per condition from one experiment). Similar fluorescence levels were observed at both developmental stages, indicating that reduced root size does not explain the increased ERS-associated expression observed under water-deficit conditions. Scale bar = 50 μm. For a, b and e, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

Gene co-expression modules. Composition of the 25 gene modules presented in Fig. 2b. The grey module contains genes that were not assigned to any co-expression module in the hdWGCNA analysis.

#2070. Ethics complaint filed against judge who had attorney jailed, shackled for ‘attitude’

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

A former Massachusetts state court judge had a public defender jailed and placed in handcuffs and shackles for hours over his “attitude” during a hearing last year, the state’s judicial conduct commission said. (Image from Shutterstock)

A former Massachusetts state court judge had a public defender jailed and placed in handcuffs and shackles for hours over his “attitude” during a hearing last year, the state’s judicial conduct commission said.

The Massachusetts Committee for Public Counsel Services filed a complaint against retired Massachusetts Justice Franco J. GoBourne in the Fall River District Court. GoBourne retired from the bench in September 2025, three months after the complaint was filed, Law360 reports.

In the complaint released Thursday, the Massachusetts Commission on Judicial Conduct alleged that GoBourne engaged in “willful judicial misconduct” that brought the office into disrepute, was prejudicial to the administration of justice and was unbecoming of a judicial officer.

GoBourne was presiding over a March 25, 2025, session in which attorney Nicholas Athanassiou was representing a client on a discovery matter. The judge had asked Athanassiou, who momentarily turned away from the judge, to stop pacing.

Athanassiou attempted to address the judge about his client’s case for several minutes before GoBourne told the lawyer to “be quiet” and ordered court officers to take him into custody for contempt, according to the complaint. Athanassiou spent five hours in handcuffs and shackles in the courthouse lockup, the complaint said.

GoBourne was listed as representing himself on an electronic case docket. He could not be reached for comment by Law360.

#2064. Former Florida Bar president faces malpractice suit in estate case

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The former president of the Florida Bar is facing a lawsuit that accuses him of malpractice in handling an estate worth an estimated $350 million. (Image from Shutterstock)

A former president of the Florida Bar is facing a lawsuit that accuses him of malpractice in handling an estate worth an estimated $350 million.

The complaint was filed Friday in the Miami-Dade Circuit Court on behalf of Mercedes del Carmen Piedra Rodriguez, a widow claiming that Roland Sanchez-Medina’s poor handling of her case cost her the full share of her late husband’s estate.

Sanchez-Medina, a partner at Sanchez-Medina, Gonzalez, Quesada, Lage, Gomez & Machado, is accused of executing a “botched” revocation of two prenuptial agreements and committing a conflict of interest by representing both husband and wife.

The law firm and two former associates were also sued, according to a story by Law.com.

Sanchez-Medina was the president of the Florida Bar from 2024 to 2025.

#2056. Athletic conference pushes back on university’s recruiting claims over ‘hallucinated’ case citations

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Disputing claims of “hallucinated” case citations in a lawsuit challenging Louisiana State University’s attempts to add former professional players to its sports teams, a collegiate athletic conference asked an Alabama court Thursday to strike the allegations. (Photo from Shutterstock)

Disputing claims of “hallucinated” case citations in a lawsuit challenging Louisiana State University’s attempts to add former professional players to its sports teams, a collegiate athletic conference asked an Alabama court Thursday to strike the allegations.

In September, the Southeastern Conference sued Louisiana State University regarding attempts to include players on its football team who previously had joined NFL training camps, asking the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of Alabama to affirm its right to impose penalties under the First Amendment.

The school did not include the former NFL players, but the suit has continued, as the Southeastern Conference asserts that Louisiana State University pushed the NCAA eligibility boundaries by courting former professional basketball players for its men’s team.

Three of the conference’s citations in a recent filing “appear to be negligently supplied or hallucinated,” according to Louisiana State University, saying the cases cut against the conference’s argument regarding the court’s jurisdiction over the case.

On Thursday, the Southeastern Conference pushed back, explaining its citations in greater detail and called on the court to correct the record.

#2055. A single archaeon couples long-chain fatty acid oxidation to methanogenesis

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

Anaerobic degradation of complex organic compounds to methane and carbon dioxide is common in the deep subsurface biosphere and has long been considered to require syntrophic cooperation, with methanogens confined to the terminal use of simple C1 and C2 substrates. Thermodynamic considerations, multi-omics analyses and microcosm experiments suggest that archaeal lineages are capable of methanogenic degradation of complex substrates1,2,3,4,5,6. However, experimental validation in pure culture was lacking. Here we report the isolation of a thermophilic archaeon, Methanoglobus dolicholipophagus AA-75, affiliated with Archaeoglobi, from a Chinese petroleum reservoir. Strain AA-75 not only converts methanol into methane, carbon dioxide and acetate, but also uses saturated fatty acids varying from C7:0 to C18:0 and unsaturated fatty acids including C16:1, C18:1 and C18:2 to produce methane. We term the latter lipotrophic methanogenesis, which integrates β-oxidation of fatty acids with the Wood–Ljungdahl pathway and the canonical methyl-tetrahydromethanopterin:coenzyme M methyltransferase and methyl-coenzyme M reductase complexes, together with an intracellular electron transfer system. A formaldehyde-methanol branch probably functions as an auxiliary route for C1 metabolism during methanogenesis. Methane produced from stearate is moderately 13C-enriched (δ13C–CH4 = −40.0‰ to −36.3‰), distinct from the strong 13C depletion characteristic of canonical methanogenic pathways. These findings demonstrate that methanogens can function as primary degraders of complex organic matter, challenging the classical syntrophic paradigm and calling for refinement of current models of carbon cycling in the deep subsurface biosphere.

#2050. 5th Circuit blasts federal judge for AI errors in rulings

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

U.S. District Judge Henry T. Wingate of the Southern District of Mississippi in August 2022. (Photo by Rogelio V. Solis/The Associated Press)

Suspecting artificial intelligence-generated errors, a federal appeals panel has overturned a preliminary injunction barring a Mississippi law that prohibits diversity, equity and inclusion in public schools and universities and ordered the case to be reassigned to a different judge on remand.

In an Oct. 22 opinion, Judge Jerry Smith of the 5th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals at New Orleans criticized the district court proceedings, noting that the temporary restraining order by U.S. District Judge Henry T. Wingate of the Southern District of Mississippi enjoining enforcement of the law “contained several factual assertions nowhere to be found in the record, or indeed, in any proceeding of the case.”

Smith noted that this included mention of plaintiffs and defendants who were not in the complaint, “declarations” from people who did not exist or were never present, and false quotations that were supposedly taken from the text of the law. He also said when these errors were discovered, Wingate replaced the order without an explanation.

Wingate later acknowledged that a law clerk used AI to draft the order, and that it was docketed by mistake, according to a story by Courthouse News Service. However, the appeals panel said the judge’s corrected version of the order also contained hallucinated information.

“Given that the preliminary injunction order ‘generally incorporates by reference its discussion and analysis in its TRO,’ the hallucinated case discussed above is arguably part of the shoddy reasoning of the preliminary injunction, too,” Smith said in the opinion.

#2037. WEE1 opposes APC/C<sup>CDH1</sup> activity to promote S phase entry

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

WEE1 phosphorylates and inhibits the activity of cyclin-dependent kinases to prevent mitotic entry and establish a G2 DNA damage checkpoint1,2,3. Consequently, WEE1 inhibitors have been developed to be synthetically lethal in TP53-mutated tumours that become reliant on this WEE1-mediated checkpoint, and such inhibitors are undergoing clinical trials. However, WEE1 inhibitors also have efficacy in TP53-wild-type tumours, and many trials have been terminated owing to high levels of toxic side effects, suggesting that WEE1 has unknown functions. Here we show that WEE1 promotes cell cycle re-entry from quiescence (G0) by opposing the activity of the E3 ubiquitin ligase, anaphase-promoting complex–cyclosome bound to CDH1 (APC/CCDH1). WEE1 phosphorylates CDH1 (FZR1) at key residues that mediate the interaction between CDH1 and APC/C. Cells with loss of function of WEE1 during G0/G1 have delayed S phase entry, impaired G1/S transition, abnormal S phase accumulation of cyclin-dependent kinase inhibitor p21Cip1/Kip1 and a p21-dependent G2 arrest. Reduced expression of APC/CCDH1 or p21 renders cells more sensitive to acute WEE1 inhibition, and both pathways are downregulated in cells with acquired WEE1 inhibitor resistance. These results reveal a mechanism of cell cycle control that has implications for how WEE1 inhibitors should be used in the clinic.

Cyclin-dependent kinases (CDKs) control passage through the eukaryotic cell cycle. WEE1 (ref. 4) is a tyrosine kinase that inhibits CDK1 by phosphorylating Tyr15 (refs. 1,2,3). Late in G2, CDC25 phosphatases dephosphorylate Tyr15, promoting mitotic entry1,5,6,7,8. This mechanism forms a G2 DNA damage checkpoint, which becomes vital in TP53-mutated cancer cells9. Thus, WEE1 inhibitors (WEE1i) have been developed to be synthetically lethal in TP53-mutated cancers and are being trialled across several indications10,11. However, toxic side effects of unknown origin11 have led to trial suspensions. WEE1 also inhibits CDK2, a key driver of G1/S progression and proliferative commitment. Consistently, non-phosphorylatable CDK2T14A;Y15F knock-in mutant mice and human colorectal cancer cells enter S phase faster when returning to the cycle from quiescence12,13. However, no direct cellular evidence of the impact of WEE1 on CDK2 is available14, and exogenous WEE1 expression in IMR90 human fibroblasts has no effect on G1/S timing15. Therefore, we sought to clarify the function of WEE1 in early cell cycle control. We demonstrate a role for WEE1 in suppressing the anaphase-promoting complex–cyclosome bound to CDH1 (APC/CCDH1), a key antagonist of G1/S progression, to promote timely cell cycle re-entry following release from quiescence. We identify CDH1 (FZR1) as a WEE1 substrate relevant to cell cycle control. Our findings shed light on the pleiotropic impact of WEE1i in cells.

To understand the potential roles of WEE1 in regulation of CDK2 activity during G1 and S phase entry, we constructed an influence diagram describing the known interactions of WEE1 with the G1/S regulatory network (Fig. 1a). WEE1 inhibits cyclin E–CDK2 and cyclin A–CDK2 (ref. 16). We converted this influence diagram to a series of ordinary differential equations and parametrized the model to reproduce the known dynamics of the G1/S regulatory network (Fig. 1b). We simulated the depletion of WEE1 levels to 50% of baseline and observed that CDK2 was activated earlier and S phase entry was faster than in the presence of 100% WEE1 activity.

a, Influence diagram of the G1/S regulatory network. b, Time-course simulation of the ordinary differential equation model demonstrating how reduction of WEE1 activity to 50% of WT levels predicts a faster increase in CDK2 activity and a shorter G1 phase. Cyclin Etotal refers to Tyr15-phosphorylated (inactive) and Tyr15-unphosphorylated (active) CDK2 combined (blue dashed curve), whereas cyclin E is Tyr15-unphosphorylated CDK2 only (active, blue solid curve). The same logic applies to cyclin A in green. The G1/S transition, marked with a grey dashed line, was determined by the time of APC/CCDH1 switch-off (red solid curve) and increase in cyclin A–CDK2. c, Length of G1 phase (left) and fraction of cells that did not enter S phase (right) after WEE1i treatment upon release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest. d, Length of G1 phase (left) and fraction of cells that did not enter S phase (right) after WEE1 depletion by siRNA 6 h before release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest. e, Length of G1 phase (left) and fraction of cells that did not enter S phase (right) after WEE1 degradation by addition of doxycycline (Dox) and IAA upon release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest. In c–e, the experimental timeline is shown above the graphs; all experiments involved n = 2 biological replicates; the mean ± s.d. are plotted with black lines; and individual data points are shown in blue or grey, coloured by experimental repeat. Significance was determined by unpaired Student’s t-test; ****P < 0.0001, **P < 0.01.

To experimentally test whether we could observe accelerated S phase entry in cells when WEE1 activity was reduced, we used AZD1775, a WEE1i previously used in clinical trials (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). To investigate the role of WEE1 in S phase entry while avoiding confounding effects from WEE1 perturbation during the preceding G2 phase, we synchronized hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cells in early G0/G1 phase with a CDK4–CDK6 inhibitor (CDK4/6i) (Extended Data Fig. 1d). Labelling of PCNA at the endogenous locus with mRuby generates a robust marker to define the G1/S transition (Methods). Subsequently, we washed out the CDK4/6i, releasing cells into G1, in the presence or absence of WEE1i and quantified G1 length by time-lapse imaging of mRuby-labelled PCNA (Extended Data Fig. 1e). Contrary to our model predictions, WEE1 inhibition increased both G1 length and the fraction of cells that did not enter S phase during the 48 h imaging period (Fig. 1c). To confirm that this was not an artefact of CDK4/6i treatment, we also arrested cells in G0 by contact inhibition, followed by serum starvation (Extended Data Fig. 1f), then replated cells at low density, releasing the cells into G1 in the presence or absence of WEE1i, and again performed time-lapse imaging. Again, we observed increased G1 length and an increased fraction of cells not entering S phase after WEE1 inhibition (Extended Data Fig. 1g).

As kinase inhibitors can have off-target effects, we depleted WEE1 from CDK4/6i-arrested G0/G1 cells using short interfering RNA (siRNA) (Extended Data Fig. 1h), then released the cells back into the cell cycle and quantified G1 length by time-lapse imaging. WEE1 depletion had a more severe impact than WEE1 inhibition, with very few cells entering S phase over the 48 h imaging period compared with control-depleted cells and those that did so having an increased G1 length (Fig. 1d). To be certain that the delay in entering S phase was due to loss of WEE1 function, we generated a cell line with an inducible WEE1–GFP degron (Extended Data Fig. 2) to remove endogenous WEE1 in a highly specific manner. We validated WEE1–GFP localization and normal growth of this cell line and showed that WEE1 could be degraded within 1 h of addition of doxycycline and indole-3-acetic acid (IAA) (Extended Data Fig. 2b,d,e). Degradation of endogenous WEE1 upon release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest again led to most cells not entering S phase; of those that did enter S phase, the length of G1 was significantly longer in the absence of WEE1 (Fig. 1e).

These data show that, contrary to our predictions and previous understanding of the functions of WEE1, WEE1 inhibition does not accelerate S phase entry. Instead, WEE1 is required for timely S phase entry in cells returning to proliferation from quiescence.

Our model (Fig. 1a,b) predicted an earlier and more rapid increase in CDK2 activity upon WEE1 inhibition. To test this, we used an exogenously expressed fluorescent live-cell reporter (CDK2L–GFP) that measures cyclin E–CDK2 activity in real time during G1 (ref. 17) (Fig. 2a). hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA CDK2L–GFP cells were released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest in the presence or absence of WEE1i, and CDK2 activity was tracked by time-lapse imaging. We used a WEE1i to perturb WEE1, as it was a clinically relevant drug, and because a higher fraction of cells entered S phase with this approach compared with siRNA or degron approaches, allowing us to make statistically meaningful comparisons. When WEE1 was inhibited, CDK2 activity started to increase earlier during G1 and reached a higher activity than in dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO)-treated cells (Fig. 2b and Supplementary Video 1). Cells treated with WEE1i therefore entered S phase at a higher level of CDK2 activity. The observation that cells could remain in G1 with high CDK2 activity was surprising; we repeated the experiment using a similar, shorter version of the CDK2 sensor (DHB–mCherry) and observed the same result18 (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b).

a, Graphic of CDK2 activity sensor. b, CDK2 activity plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval from 1 of n = 2 biological repeats. a.u., arbitrary units. c, Timing and rate of APC/CCDH1 inactivation plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). G1/S was defined by mTurquoise2–PCNA. Change in mean over time is shown. Mean and 95% confidence interval of n = 2 biological repeats. Extra sum-of-squares F test comparing Hill functions fitted to the two curves (DMSO versus WEE1i) determined no significant difference. d, CDK2 and APC/CCDH1 activity plotted together for comparison of behaviour around G1/S (individual experiments in b and c). Data were in silico-aligned to G1/S, on the basis of fluorescent PCNA in each cell line. Top, DMSO-treated cells; bottom, WEE1i-treated cells. e, Top, protein levels of nuclear CDC6–mVenus plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval from 1 of n = 3 biological repeats. Bottom, measurements of the licensing window, defined by nuclear CDC6–mVenus protein accumulation (left), and peak nuclear CDC6–mVenus intensity (right). Mean ± s.d. are plotted in black, individual data points are coloured by experimental repeat. Significance was determined by unpaired Student’s t-test; ****P < 0.0001. CDC6–mVenus is rapidly exported to the cytoplasm at the end of G1 (ref. 25), hence the rapid drop in nuclear intensity. f, Quantification of chromatin-bound MCM7 at time points after release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (grey) or WEE1i (red). Mean ± s.d. from one of n = 2 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) followed by Šídák’s multiple comparisons test; ****P < 0.0001; NS, not significant.

Two key events drive the G1/S transition: an increase in CDK2 activity and inhibition of APC/CCDH1 E3 ubiquitin ligase activity19 (Fig. 1b). CDK2 can phosphorylate CDH1 and inhibit APC/CCDH1 activity20,21,22. Therefore, we tested whether there was any change in the timing and/or rate of APC/CCDH1 inactivation relative to the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells that had abnormally high CDK2 activity in G1. We used hTert-RPE1 cells expressing mCherry–geminin(1–110), constitutively expressed from an exogenous promoter, as a live-cell reporter of APC/CCDH1 activity21. Geminin is very effectively degraded by APC/CCDH1 during G1, and geminin protein only starts to accumulate after the G1/S transition (Extended Data Fig. 3c). Therefore, mCherry–geminin, together with coexpressed mTurquoise2–PCNA (under the control of an exogenous promoter), is a useful reporter for determining the timing and rate of APC/CCDH1 inactivation relative to the G1/S transition. Using this reporter, we identified that in cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, APC/CCDH1 activity was switched off just after the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells, with timing and rate indistinguishable from those observed in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2c and Extended Data Fig. 3c). Our data suggest that upon WEE1 inhibition, CDK2 activity increases to a higher level in G1, but the timing of APC/CCDH1 inactivation relative to the G1/S transition remains similar to that in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2d). Consequently, APC/CCDH1 inactivation is more important than reaching a threshold of CDK2 activity with respect to establishing the timing of S phase entry.

Low CDK2 activity during G1 provides a period for licensing of DNA replication origins23. An early increase in CDK2 activity in G1 in WEE1i-treated cells could decrease DNA replication origin licensing. CDC6 is an essential replication origin licensing factor that is required for formation of prereplicative complexes at replication origins24. We used an hTert-RPE1 cell line expressing mTurquoise2–PCNA, DHB–mCherry and CDC6–mVenus25, all under the control of exogenous constitutively active promoters, to track the dynamics of nuclear CDC6 protein relative to the G1/S transition. In cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest in the presence of WEE1i, the licensing window (the time from the rise to the peak in nuclear CDC6–mVenus intensity25) was reduced compared with that in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2e). We also quantified replication origin licensing during G1 by quantifying chromatin-bound MCM protein in cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest. We observed a decrease in chromatin-bound MCM7 protein in WEE1i-treated G1 cells compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2f).

In summary, these results demonstrate that WEE1 inhibits CDK2 activity during G1, and that an early and rapid increase in CDK2 activity after WEE1 inhibition can lead to reduced DNA replication origin licensing but not inhibition of APC/CCDH1 activity. Increased CDK2 activity is insufficient to accelerate the transition of WEE1i-treated G1 cells into S phase, suggesting that WEE1 has other roles during G1.

As progression through G1 was abnormal in WEE1i-treated cells, we examined whether such cells entered S phase normally. We observed that mRuby–PCNA localization at the start of S phase was abnormal in WEE1i-treated cells, with low PCNA intensity and less well-defined PCNA foci compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3a). In WT cells, as S phase progresses, mRuby–PCNA localization changes, with early DNA replication factories (small puncta) coalescing into larger DNA replication factories (large foci). Thus, the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA intensity normally increases throughout S phase. We quantified the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA nuclear intensity; this showed a clear reduction at the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3b). This decrease also remained throughout S phase, indicating altered S phase progression in the presence of WEE1i, consistent with previous reports26,27,28.

a, Images of mRuby–PCNA from DMSO-treated (top) and WEE1i-treated (bottom) cells. b, Quantification of the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA intensity (Methods), relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest into DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n > 3 biological repeats are shown. c, Quantification of PIP-mVenus intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest into DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. One-phase exponential decay is denoted by dotted lines, and the half-life for each condition is shown. d, Quantification of p21–GFP nuclear intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (control, black line) or WEE1i (red line). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. e, Quantification of p21–GFP nuclear intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of WEE1i (red) or combined WEE1i and CDK1i (yellow). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. f, Quantification of nuclear γH2Ax intensity in EdU-positive cells released from G0/G1 arrest for 48 h in the presence of DMSO or WEE1i. Significance was determined by Student’s t-test; ****P < 0.0001. Data from 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. g, Cell cycle phase timings for p21-WT and p21-knockout (KO) cells treated with DMSO (left) or WEE1i (right) after release from CDK4/6i. Data from one of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. h, Quantification of G1 or G2 arrest in p21-WT and p21-KO cells treated with DMSO or WEE1i. Mean ± s.d. of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA; *P < 0.05, ***P < 0.001. Scale bars, 20 μm.

PCNA recruitment to chromatin in S phase brings substrates, including DNA licensing factor CDT1 and CDK inhibitor protein p21, in contact with the CRL4CDT2 E3 ubiquitin ligase. This occurs through substrate binding to PCNA through a PCNA interaction protein (PIP) motif29, which leads to rapid substrate degradation and S phase progression. We assayed whether the change in PCNA chromatin recruitment in WEE1i-treated cells affected PIP-containing substrate degradation using an hTert-RPE1 cell line expressing the PIP-FUCCI reporter from an exogenous promoter and time-lapse imaging. We tracked the dynamics of the degradation of this construct, which contains the PIP motif of CDT1 fused to an mVenus fluorophore, at the G1/S transition. In WEE1i-treated cells, the degradation of PIP-mVenus was slower compared with that in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3c), suggesting that impaired recruitment of PCNA to chromatin at the start of S phase affects the rate of degradation of PIP-containing substrates.

p21 contains a PIP motif; therefore, p21 protein is normally rapidly degraded at S phase entry30,31,32, remains low throughout S phase and reaccumulates at the S/G2 transition when CRL4CDT2 is inhibited by CDK1 (ref. 33). As PIP-mVenus was more slowly degraded at the G1/S transition after WEE1 inhibition, we proposed that normal p21 protein degradation might also be impaired. To test this hypothesis, we used an hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cell line expressing endogenously labelled p21–GFP34. We observed that in WEE1i-treated cells, p21–GFP protein accumulated abnormally during S phase (Fig. 3d, Extended Data Fig. 4a,b and Supplementary Video 2). This suggested continuing impaired p21 degradation in S phase, not just at the G1/S transition, upon WEE1 inhibition. We further proposed that WEE1 inhibition in S phase would lead to increased CDK1 activity and premature inactivation of CRL4CDT2 (ref. 33). We tested whether treating cells with a CDK1 inhibitor would restore CRL4CDT2 activity during S phase and prevent accumulation of p21. Combining WEE1 and CDK1 inhibition prevented accumulation of p21–GFP in S phase cells treated with WEE1i alone (Fig. 3e and Extended Data Fig. 4c,d).

If p21 is not degraded during S phase, it can cause severe disruption to DNA replication and DNA damage35. Cells treated with WEE1i had increased levels of DNA damage compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3f and Extended Data Fig. 4e). These results indicate that abnormal p21 protein accumulation may contribute to DNA damage in WEE1i-treated cells.

High levels of p21 protein can cause cell cycle arrest in G2. We tracked the cell cycle fate of hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest and treated with WEE1i. We observed an increase in the fraction of cells arresting in G2 in WEE1i-treated cells compared with DMSO-treated cells and determined that this was p21-dependent (Fig. 3g,h), whereas the increase in G1 arrest after WEE1i treatment was not p21-dependent (Fig. 3g,h). WEE1i-treated cells do not completely replicate their DNA during S phase; therefore, more accurately, this was a ‘G2-like’ arrest36. We confirmed that this was the case by examining cellular DNA content: we observed a large accumulation of cells between 2n and 4n specifically in WEE1i-treated cells (Extended Data Fig. 4f,g).

These data show that WEE1 inhibition leads to an abnormal G1/S transition that is exacerbated by the detrimental effects of WEE1 on DNA replication.

We next aimed to determine the mechanism preventing WEE1-inhibited G1 cells with high CDK2 activity from entering S phase prematurely. As APC/CCDH1 seemed to be inactivated with normal rate and timing around the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells, we considered whether WEE1 inhibition could cause increased or prolonged APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 that could lead to a delay in S phase entry. This hypothesis was consistent with a previous report showing that WEE1 physically interacts with and can inhibit the ubiquitin ligase activity of APC/CCDH1 (ref. 37).

First, we revised our ordinary differential equation model to test the impact of WEE1 inhibition of APC/CCDH1 on the G1/S regulatory network (Fig. 4a). We considered three hypotheses for this interaction: first, that WEE1 inactivates CDH1 through a mechanism identical to that of CDK2 (‘CDK2-like model’), that is, WEE1 phosphorylates the same sites in CDH1 as in CDK2 (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b); second, that WEE1 does not inactivate CDH1 directly but rather increases the affinity of cyclin E–CDK2 for APC/CCDH1 (‘threshold model’), which has otherwise been shown to be negligible in vitro20,38 (Extended Data Fig. 5e,f); and, third, that WEE1 inactivates CDH1 independently of CDK2, by phosphorylating separate amino acid residues in CDH1 (‘independent model’) (Extended Data Fig. 5i,j).

a, Revised influence diagram for the G1/S network. b, Simulation from the revised ‘independent’ model. G1/S (grey) is determined by APC/CCDH1 switch-off and cyclin A–CDK2 increase. c, Quantification of CDC25A–GFP nuclear intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest into DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats. Right, quantification of peak CDC25A–GFP intensity. Mean ± s.d., n = 2 biological repeats. Significance was determined by unpaired Student’s t-test; ***P < 0.001. d, G1 length in cells released from G0/G1 arrest under the conditions labelled. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA followed by Tukey’s multiple comparisons test; ****P < 0.0001. e, Quantification of G0/G1 arrest in cells released from G0/G1 arrest under the conditions labelled. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA followed by Tukey’s multiple comparisons test; ****P < 0.0001. In c–e, individual data points are coloured by experimental repeat. f, Immunoblot of in vitro kinase assay for WEE1 and CDH1. One of n = 2 biological repeats. g, APC/CCDH1 structure (PDB9GAW); the hydrogen bond between CDH1 Tyr91 and APC1 His1231 is highlighted. h, APC/CCDH1 structure; the intramolecular hydrogen bonds between CDH1 Tyr130 and Asp46 and Arg47 are highlighted. i, Cartoon showing how WEE1 phosphorylation of CDH1 could affect CDH1 binding and APC/CCDH1 activity. j, Left, CDH1 binding assay with WT and phosphomimetic CDH1. Endogenous APC/C was immunoprecipitated from Xenopus egg extracts. Right, quantification of CDH1 immunoblot, normalized to APC5 then to APC/C-bound WT CDH1. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. k, Left, APC/CCDH1-dependent destruction assay. Right, quantification of cyclin B levels over time, normalized to cyclin B Δ67, then to 0 min. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. The Thr129Asp CDH1 mutation is included as this site has been shown to be phosphorylated by mTOR43. NTC, non-targeting control.

All three models reproduced G0 arrest upon complete WEE1 depletion (Fig. 1d,e and Extended Data Fig. 5d,h,l). However, they differed markedly with respect to the emergent APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 and at G1/S upon partial (50%) WEE1 inhibition. Thus, each set of assumptions made distinct, experimentally verifiable predictions. The threshold model predicted a gradual inactivation of APC/CCDH1 around the G1/S transition (Extended Data Fig. 5f), which was inconsistent with our time-lapse data (Fig. 2c). This left the CDK2-like and independent models of CDH1 inhibition by WEE1. These two models differed principally in terms of the change in APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 after WEE1 inhibition (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b,i,j). In the CDK2-like model, APC/CCDH1 activity remained unchanged after WEE1 inhibition, although it was prolonged, whereas in the independent model, APC/CCDH1 activity increased after WEE1 inhibition. To differentiate between the two possibilities, we quantified levels of APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 in WEE1i-treated cells.

To estimate APC/CCDH1 activity in G1 cells, we used time-lapse imaging to quantify the levels of two APC/CCDH1 substrates that accumulate during G1: CDC6 (ref. 39) and CDC25A (ref. 40). We considered using the mCherry–geminin reporter, but as geminin is very efficiently degraded by APC/CCDH1, the mCherry–geminin signal intensity was negligible in G1 (Extended Data Fig. 3c). Quantification of the peak intensity of nuclear CDC6–mVenus protein from our previous analysis showed that CDC6–mVenus protein levels were decreased in WEE1i-treated G1 cells compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2e). This was owing to decreased CDC6–mVenus protein stability in WEE1i-treated cells (Extended Data Fig. 3d,e). To analyse CDC25A protein levels in real time, we generated an hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cell line in which we introduced a GFP fluorophore into the carboxyl terminus of the endogenous CDC25A gene (Extended Data Fig. 6a). We validated these cells (Extended Data Fig. 6b–f). In unperturbed cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, CDC25A–GFP showed accumulation around the G1/S transition (Fig. 4c and Supplementary Video 3). We confirmed that CDC25A–GFP was a reliable readout of APC/CCDH1 activity in G1 by depleting CDH1 using siRNA, then quantifying CDC25A–GFP levels and showing that they were increased (Extended Data Fig. 6g). After WEE1i treatment in cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, CDC25A–GFP levels were much reduced, compared with those in DMSO-treated cells, around the G1/S transition (Fig. 4c). This reduction was due to a change in CDC25A protein stability, rather than a change in CDC25A transcription (Extended Data Fig. 6h,i). The decreased CDC6 and CDC25A protein levels after WEE1i treatment during G1 were consistent with increased APC/CCDH1 activity after WEE1 inhibition and thus the independent model of WEE1 inhibition of CDH1 (Fig. 4b and Extended Data Fig. 5i–l).

To test whether increased APC/CCDH1 activity caused a longer G1 phase in WEE1i-treated cells, we used siRNA to deplete CDH1. This led to a short G1 length (Fig. 4d and Extended Data Fig. 7a). CDH1 depletion and simultaneous inhibition of WEE1 also led to a short G1 length, consistent with our model and supporting the hypothesis that in WEE1i-treated cells, APC/CCDH1 causes a prolonged G1 phase (Fig. 4d). Using an hTert-RPE1 cell line expressing exogenous, doxycycline-inducible EMI1 to inhibit APC/CCDH1 (ref. 41), we observed a robust decrease in CDH1 protein levels and a decrease in the proportion of G0/G1-arrested cells in the presence of WEE1i (Fig. 4e and Extended Data Fig. 7b). Notably, we did not observe any increase in CDH1 protein levels or change in CDH1 stability in G1 cells after WEE1i treatment (Extended Data Fig. 7c–e), suggesting that WEE1 does not regulate CDH1 protein stability.

To test whether WEE1 phosphorylated CDH1 directly, we used in vitro kinase reactions with purified WEE1 and CDH1 proteins. WEE1 phosphorylated tyrosine(s) in CDH1 (Fig. 4f and Extended Data Fig. 7f) in a time-dependent manner, whereas phosphorylation of related APC/C coactivator CDC20 was negligible (Extended Data Fig. 7g). Mass spectrometry analysis showed that Tyr91 and Tyr130 of CDH1 were phosphorylated by WEE1. We re-analysed the samples using a targeted liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry approach, focusing acquisition on the identified phosphorylated peptides. This provided exceptionally high-quality fragmentation data, allowing unambiguous localization of the phosphorylation sites and demonstrating phosphorylation of Tyr91 and Tyr130 of CDH1 by WEE1 (Extended Data Fig. 7h,i). Tyr91Phe–Tyr130Phe CDH1 mutants had substantially reduced WEE1-driven pTyr phosphorylation, indicating that these sites contribute substantially to CDH1 phosphorylation, although WEE1 may phosphorylate other sites (Extended Data Fig. 7j). These sites, particularly Tyr130, are conserved across species (Extended Data Fig. 8), and both phosphorylation events have previously been detected in cells, although the kinase responsible was not identified42,43.

Scrutinizing the structure of human APC/CCDH1 (ref. 44), we found that the Tyr91 and Tyr130 sites were part of a CDH1 amino-terminal extension that interacted extensively with APC/C. Tyr91 sat at an interface between CDH1 and APC1 and formed a hydrogen bond with His1231 in APC1 (Fig. 4g). Tyr130 formed intramolecular hydrogen bonds with Asp46 and Arg47 and seemed to stabilize the C-box motif in CDH1 (ref. 45) (DRFIP), which has a crucial role in maintenance of the interaction with APC8b (ref. 46) (Fig. 4h). These observations suggest that phosphorylation of CDH1 at Tyr91 and Tyr130 could weaken the engagement of CDH1 with APC/C and thereby reduce APC/CCDH1 activity (Fig. 4i). To test this, we examined whether phosphomimetic substitution of the WEE1-targeted residues affected APC/C binding by CDH1. In APC/C binding assays in Xenopus extract, both CDH1 Tyr91Glu and CDH1 Tyr130Glu showed reduced association with APC/C, with Tyr130Glu consistently causing the stronger defect. Notably, the CDH1 Tyr91Glu–Tyr130Glu double mutant was almost completely defective in APC/C binding (Fig. 4j). Consistent with this binding defect, in vitro cyclin destruction assays showed that CDH1 Tyr130Glu was impaired in APC/C activation, whereas CDH1 Tyr91Glu caused only a modest defect. The double phosphomimetic mutant was severely compromised (Fig. 4k). These findings support a model in which WEE1-mediated phosphorylation of CDH1 inhibits APC/CCDH1 by weakening CDH1 engagement with APC/C.

On the basis of our data and modelling, we propose a mechanism of G1/S control whereby WEE1 phosphorylates and inhibits APC/CCDH1, independently of CDK2-mediated inhibition of CDH1, to control G1 length and promote robust S phase entry.

Having shown that WEE1 may suppress APC/CCDH1 activity and maintain low levels of p21 protein during S phase, we next investigated the implications of these findings for WEE1i treatment in the clinic. To determine whether WEE1 inhibition resulted in similar phenotypes in cancer cells to those observed in non-transformed hTert-RPE1 cells, we repeated experiments in TP53-wild-type (WT) KU1919 bladder cancer and MCF7 breast cancer cell lines engineered to express endogenously labelled mRuby–PCNA. These cell lines were chosen as they have a functional p53–p21 pathway and are sensitive to CDK4/6i, which enabled us to robustly arrest cells in G0/G1. G1 length increased in WEE1i-treated KU1919 and MCF7 cells, and cells of both lines underwent significant G1 and G2 arrest after treatment with WEE1i, similar to phenotypes of hTert-RPE1 cells (Fig. 5a,b and Extended Data Fig. 9a–f). G2 arrest was p21-dependent, and we observed increased cell death during G2 in WEE1i-treated cells compared with DMSO-treated cells in the absence of p21 in all cell lines tested (Figs. 3g and 5a,c and Extended Data Fig. 9a). These data indicate that cells lacking p21 may be more sensitive to WEE1 inhibition. CDKN1A (encoding p21) is rarely mutated in cancers, except in bladder cancer, for which between 10% and 15% of patients have predicted loss-of-function mutations of p21 (ref. 47). These mutations occur early and can be present in normal bladders48. Therefore, we investigated whether WEE1i treatment could be used to eradicate pretumorigenic CDKN1A mutant lesions to prevent a subset of bladder cancers. We generated p21-knockout cell lines in the non-transformed bladder urothelial cell line HBLAK and found that these cells were indeed more sensitive to WEE1i than their WT counterparts (Fig. 5d and Extended Data Fig. 9g).

a, Cell cycle phase timings for p21-WT and p21-KO KU1919 bladder cancer cells treated with DMSO (left) or WEE1i (right), after release from CDK4/6i. Data from 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. b, Fractions of KU1919 cells arrested in G1 or G2 phase in p21-WT and p21-KO cells treated with DMSO or WEE1i. Mean ± s.d., n = 2 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA; **P < 0.01, ***P < 0.001. c, Percentages of cells dying, quantified by time-lapse imaging, in p21-WT and p21-KO hTert-RPE1, MCF7 and KU1919 cells treated with DMSO or WEE1i. Mean ± s.d., n = 2 biological repeats. Multiple unpaired t-tests were performed for comparison: *P < 0.05, **P < 0.001. d, Dose–response curves showing sensitivity of p21-WT and p21-KO (two clones, C1 and C2) non-transformed HBLAK bladder cell lines to WEE1i treatment (extra sum-of-squares F test, p21 WT versus p21-KO: P < 0.0001). Half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values: WT 0.38 μM, p21-KO C1 0.18 μM, p21-KO C2 0.21 μM. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. e, Dose–response curves showing sensitivity of cells treated with non-targeting control (grey) or CDH1-targeting (blue) siRNA, 24 h before treatment with WEE1i (extra sum-of-squares F test; P < 0.0001). IC50 values: non-targeting control 0.2 μM, siCDH1 0.11 μM. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. f, Dose–response curves showing sensitivity of parental (sensitive) and WEE1i-resistant MCF7 cells to WEE1i treatment (extra sum-of-squares F test, parental versus WEE1i-resistant; P < 0.0001). IC50 values: sensitive 0.1 μM, resistant 0.49 μM. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. g, Genes significantly upregulated or downregulated (blue) in WEE1i-resistant cells compared with parental (sensitive) cells from RNA sequencing analysis. TP53, CDKN1A, ANAPC7 and ANAPC10 are highlighted. h, Schematic showing the relationships of acute and chronic WEE1 inhibition (WEE1i) with CDH1 activity and p21 levels.

We reasoned that depletion of CDH1 might also sensitize cells to WEE1i by allowing the cells to enter S phase and incur WEE1i-mediated DNA damage. Indeed, siRNA-mediated depletion of CDH1 increased sensitivity of hTert-RPE1 cells to WEE1i (Fig. 5e).

To identify changes that could drive acquired resistance to WEE1i, we generated a WEE1i-resistant MCF7 cell line (Fig. 5f). We profiled these cells by RNA sequencing and found that WEE1i-resistant cells showed upregulation of nucleotide biosynthetic pathways, consistent with previous work showing that nucleoside supplementation reduces double-strand breaks caused by increased replication initiation in WEE1i-treated cells26 (Extended Data Fig. 9h–j and Supplementary Data Table 1). WEE1i-resistant cells also showed downregulated TP53 and CDKN1A expression, as well as components of APC/CCDH1 (ANAPC7 and ANAPC10, encoding APC7 and APC10, respectively) (Fig. 5g). These observations were consistent with our mechanistic findings, as cells would need downregulation of the p53–p21 and APC/CCDH1 pathways to enable continued proliferation in the presence of WEE1i. However, loss of these proteins would lead to increased sensitivity to acute WEE1i treatment, as we observed for p21 and CDH1 (Fig. 5c,e,h). Analysis of DepMap for genes that synergized with WEE1 loss identified several APC/CCDH1 components (Extended Data Fig. 9k).

In conclusion, our data suggest that CDKN1A mutation status or APC/CCDH1 subunit expression could be used to improve patient selection and potentially reduce toxicity in WEE1i clinical trials.

The substantial side effects associated with WEE1i treatment reported in clinical trials, including myelosuppression, gastrointestinal issues and fatigue, have been attributed to potential off-target effects on PLK1. An alternative explanation is that WEE1 has other functions. We report a role for WEE1 in the regulation of re-entry into the cell cycle from quiescence. This could account for some of the effects of WEE1i observed in healthy tissues.

Our findings lead to an apparent conundrum, in that WEE1 inhibits CDK2 to prevent S phase entry yet also inhibits APC/CCDH1 to promote S phase entry. We propose that WEE1 overall promotes S phase entry. By inhibiting APC/CCDH1 activity, WEE1 permits CDC25A protein to accumulate; this in turn reverses WEE1 inhibitory phosphorylation of CDK2. WEE1 may be required for initial inhibition of APC/CCDH1 before CDK2-mediated CDH1 phosphorylation and degradation. Future work involving mutation of WEE1 and CDK phospho-sites in CDH1 and identification of tyrosine phosphatases that act on CDH1 could determine the relative importance of each pathway with respect to S phase entry. mTOR-mediated phosphorylation of CDH1 Thr129 transiently inactivates APC/CCDH1 (ref. 43). This site is adjacent to CDH1 Tyr130, which is phosphorylated by WEE1, and phosphorylation of Ser131 has also been detected in cells42; these findings indicate a potential key role for this motif in regulation of APC/CCDH1 activity, probably involving stabilization of the interaction of the C-box in CDH1 with APC8. mTOR inhibition decreased Thr129 and Tyr130 phosphorylation in cells; however, mTOR only phosphorylates Thr129 (ref. 43). Therefore, mTOR phosphorylation may precede WEE1 phosphorylation of CDH1 Tyr130. Together, these data indicate that APC/CCDH1 may function as a signal integrator in G1 to determine whether cells commit to cell proliferation.

Our experimental data are most consistent with the model in which WEE1 inhibition of APC/CCDH1 activity is independent of CDK2-mediated APC/CCDH1 inhibition. However, there remain two discrepancies. First, although both modelling and experimental data showed reduced CDC25A levels during G1 after WEE1 inhibition, the model predicts that CDC25A levels should increase after S phase entry, owing to inactivation of APC/CCDH1 at the G1/S transition. This post-G1/S increase in CDC25A does not occur in cells. This is likely to be because Cdc25A is degraded by CHK1-mediated phosphorylation in S phase cells49 (Extended Data Fig. 10a). Second, whereas the model is consistent with our data in that cells remain arrested in G0/G1 at very low levels of WEE1 activity, the model predicts high CDK2 activity and inhibition of Rb during the arrest, which we did not observe (Extended Data Fig. 10b). The reasons for this are unclear but could involve reduced CAK activity in arrested cells50.

WEE1i were designed to be synthetically lethal with TP53 mutations, which would cause DNA-damage-induced p53-mediated p21 expression to be inactive. However, WEE1i show efficacy in TP53-WT tumours; this demonstrates a need to understand the parameters that define WEE1i sensitivity. Here we identified expression of p21 and APC/CCDH1 complex components as potential indicators of sensitivity to WEE1i. Acute loss of p21 or CDH1 sensitizes cells to WEE1 inhibition, whereas reduced p53–p21 and APC/CCDH1 expression in resistant cells may reflect adaptation to chronic drug exposure. These findings indicate that CDKN1A status and APC/CCDH1 pathway activity may merit further investigation as biomarkers for WEE1i-based therapies, including in CDKN1A mutant bladder cancer. Our data also suggest that WEE1i toxicity may arise, at least in part, from increased APC/CCDH1 activity in quiescent stem cells, which impairs their ability to re-enter proliferation and maintain tissue function.

All cell lines used are listed in Supplementary Table 1.

hTert-RPE1 and MCF7 cells were cultured at 37 °C and 5% CO2 with the corresponding growth conditions: DMEM (Gibco, 11594486) supplemented with 10% fetal bovine serum (FBS; Gibco F7514) and 1% penicillin–streptomycin (Gibco 15140); KU1919 and HBLAK cells were cultured under the same environmental conditions, but the media were RPMI 1640 (Gibco, A1049101) and Cnt Prime (Cellntec, CnT-PR), respectively.

All cell lines were routinely tested for mycoplasma using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay test kit (MycoAlert, LT07-318). Cells in culture were routinely split 1:10 every 3–4 days. For cell splitting, cells were washed with phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) and detached by incubation for 3 min with Gibco Trypsin-EDTA (0.25%) and phenol red (Gibco; 25200056) before being replated in fresh media. Accutase (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 00-4555-56) was used for HBLAK cells.

hTert-RPE1 cells were arrested in G0/G1 by contact inhibition so that they reached 100% confluency for 3 days in a 6-well plate. After 6 days, cells were replated at low cell density in serum-free DMEM for 24 h, according to the protocol described in ref. 25. Cells were released into the cell cycle with addition of serum-containing DMEM (as above for growth medium).

hTert-RPE1 cells were treated with 0.5–1 µM palbociclib (Selleckchem, S4482) for 24 h to arrest cells in G0/G1 (ref. 51). Cells were washed three times with PBS to release cells back into the cell cycle with addition of full growth medium. KU1919 cells were treated with 1 µM palbociclib for 24 h, then washed three times with PBS to release cells back into the cell cycle with addition of full growth medium. MCF7 cells were treated with 1 µM palbociclib for 24 h, before being washed three times with PBS and released into the cell cycle with addition of appropriate medium plus serum.

Chemically competent E. coli DH5a cells (NEB, C2987H) were used for transformation.
