Competent cells were thawed on ice for 30 min, after which 0.5 μg of the plasmid DNA of interest was added, followed by incubation on ice for a further 20 min. To facilitate the uptake of plasmid DNA, the cells were subjected to heat shock for 45 s at 42 °C in a water bath and subsequent incubation on ice for 2 min. After this, 250 μl of room-temperature SOC medium was added to the cells, and the mixture was incubated in a horizontal shaker at 37 °C for 1 h. The bacteria were then spread on the appropriate antibiotic-containing LB agar plates and incubated at 37 °C overnight.

CDH1 mutant plasmids carrying Y91E, Y130E or T129D or combinations of these mutations are listed in Supplementary Table 2. All point mutations were introduced in the HA-CDH1 plasmid (Addgene, 11596) by Gibson assembly with Gibson Assembly Master Mix (E2611S, NEB), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. The Y91E fragments were PCR-amplified with forward primer 5′-ctggggacgtcggagcaagc-3′ and reverse primer 5′-agggcagaCTCGGCCAGGCCGTCTTTG-3′ for the insert and forward primer 5′-ggccGAGTCTGCCCTGCTCAAGAATG-3′ and reverse primer 5′-gctccgacgtccccaggcagaatgg-3′ for the backbone. The Y130E fragments were PCR-amplified with forward primer 5′-ctggggacgtcggagcaagc-3′ and reverse primer 5′-gggaCTCcgtgaacagacccttcttc-3′ for the insert and forward primer 5′-ggtctgttcacgGAGtcccttagcacca-3′ and reverse primer 5′-gctccgacgtccccaggcagaatgg-3′ for the backbone. The Y91E/Y130E fragments were PCR-amplified with forward primer 5′-ggccGAGTCTGCCCTGCTCAAGAATG-3′ and reverse primer 5′-gggaCTCcgtgaacagacccttcttc-3′ for the insert and forward primer 5′-ggtctgttcacgGAGtcccttagcacca-3′ and reverse primer 5′-agggcagaCTCGGCCAGGCCGTCTTTG-3′ for the backbone. The Y91E/T219D/Y130E fragments were PCR-amplified with forward primer 5′-ggccGAGTCTGCCCTGCTCAAGAATG-3′ and reverse primer 5′-gggaCTCGTCgaacagacccttcttctcaggc-3′ for the insert and forward primer 5′-ggtctgttcGACGAGtcccttagcacca-3′ and reverse primer 5′-agggcagaCTCGGCCAGGCCGTCTTTG-3′ for the backbone. Subsequently, CDH1 WT and mutated sequences were subcloned in the pHY22 (ref. 52) plasmid backbone by Gibson assembly. The pHY22 backbone was PCR-amplified using forward primer 5′-GGATCGATCCTCTAGAGTCGACCT-3′ and reverse primer 5′-GCCATATGCGGTAAAATAACGTTTA-3′. HA-CDH1 WT and mutated sequences were PCR-amplified using forward primer 5′-TTTACCGCATATGGCatggaccaggactatgagcg-3′ and reverse primer 5′-CTAGAGGATCGATCCttaccggatcctggtgaagagg-3′.

Plasmids expressing the N-terminal 186 residues of human CDH1 (WT, Y91F, Y130F and Y91F/Y130F) with a C-terminal His6 tag were generated by PCR amplification of the corresponding double-stranded gBlocks (Integrated DNA Technologies, BVBA) using primers containing 5′ NdeI and 3′ BamHI sites. The PCR products were digested with NdeI and BamHI and subcloned into the pET-NGST vector. All constructs were confirmed by DNA sequencing.

The GFP–AID–P2A–Neo tag (a gift from Francis Barr’s laboratory53) was inserted at the C terminus of the endogenous WEE1 coding sequence using CRISPR–Cas9-mediated editing.

gRNA cloning. The guide RNA (gRNA) used was forward 5′-ACCTCCCCTGAACACTGTG-3′, reverse 5′-CACAGTGTTCAGGGGGAGGT-3′. This gRNA sequence was cloned into pX459 pSpCas9 using the Bbsl restriction enzyme. pSpCas9(BB)−2A-Puro (PX459) V2.0 was a gift from F. Zhang (Addgene plasmid 62988)54.

Homology donor cloning. The genomic region surrounding WEE1 exon 11 (C terminus) was PCR-amplified from hTert-RPE1 genomic DNA using forward primer 5′-agtatttggcctattaaatttgtgttg-3′ and reverse primer 5′-tgcaaattgaagtgataatgttatttc-3′. WEE1 exon 11 was cloned into the pJet vector (Thermo Fisher), and the sequence was verified by Sanger sequencing. For construction of the homology donor plasmid, WEE1 homology arms were PCR-amplified from pJet–WEE1 exon 11 using high-fidelity Q5 DNA polymerase (NEB), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. For PCR amplification of the left homology arm, the following primers were used (the 5′ overhangs for Gibson assembly are underlined): forward primer 5′-acggtatcgataagcttgatattcttttgatttattgcag-3′ and reverse primer 5′-gccccaccaccgctccaccgtatatatagtaaggctgacag-3′. For PCR amplification of the right homology arm, forward primer 5′-cttcttgacgagttcttctgagactactcctttcacacctcc-3′ (the base mutated to change the PAM site in the recombined gene is shown in bold) and reverse primer 5′-ccgggctgcaggaattcgatgataaaaatatattttgtg-3′ were used. GFP–AID–GSG–P2A–Neo was PCR amplified from plasmid pFB8580 (provided by Francis Barr’s laboratory53) using forward primer 5′-ggtgggagcggtggtggcg-3′ and reverse primer 5′-tcagaagaactcgtcaagaaggcgat-3′. The pBluescript (pSKII) vector was digested with EcoRV.

All PCR products were run on 0.8% TBE agarose gels, and bands were gel extracted and assembled into EcoRV-digested pSKII(-) using Gibson assembly with a NEB HiFi Assembly kit, according to the manufacturer’s instructions for four-way assembly. The final homology donor plasmid and the pX459 plasmid containing the gRNA were sequenced using Sanger sequencing to confirm the correct sequence.

Generation of WEE1–GFP–AID-tagged cell line. For generation of the tagged cell lines, hTert-RPE1 MYC-OsTIR1 cells (expressing a doxycycline-inducible MYC–OsTIR1 construct; a gift from H. Hochegger55) were seeded in a 6-well plate at 5 × 104 cells per well in 1.5 ml of growth medium, 1 day before transfection. The next day, cells were forward transfected with Lipofectamine 2000 and 500 ng of pX459-gRNA and 500 ng of pSK-WEE1–GFP–AID–P2A–Neo. Cells were allowed to recover for 24 h before trypsinization and seeding in a 15-cm plate with fresh growth media. The next day, 1 mg ml−1 of G418 (Thermo Fisher) was added to the plate; then, fresh medium plus G418 was added every 3–4 days until single colonies became visible. Single-cell colonies were picked using cloning cylinders (Sigma). Positive clones were identified by nuclear GFP fluorescence and analysed by western blot. We confirmed that this cell line had the expected nuclear localization of WEE1–GFP (Extended Data Fig. 2b) and that it grew with normal kinetics (Extended Data Fig. 2d).

gRNA cloning. For generation of the gRNA targeting the C terminus of human CDC25A for CRISPR–Cas9-mediated gene-tagging, gRNA forward primer 5′-caccgaagaagctctgagggcggc-3′ and gRNA reverse primer 5′-aaacgccgccctcagagcttcttc-3′ were annealed and ligated into BbsI-digested pSpCas9(BB)−2A-Puro (PX459) V2.0.

Homology donor cloning. For PCR amplification of the CDC25A left and right homology arms from hTert-RPE1 genomic DNA, we used NEB Q5 High Fidelity DNA polymerase and the following primers: for the left homology arm (the 5′ overhangs for Gibson assembly are underlined), forward 5′-acggtatcgataagcttgatgtgaaggtgctgggcaggactc-3′ and reverse 5′-cgccaccaccgctcccaccgagcttcttcagacgactgtac-3′; for the right homology arm (the base mutated to remove the PAM sequence in recombined DNA is shown in bold), forward 5′-atcgccttcttgacgagttcttctgagggcggcatgaccagccagcag-3′ and reverse 5′-ccgggctgcaggaattcgatcagagcacctagctttctgtcc-3′. GFP–GSG–P2A–Neo was PCR amplified from plasmid pFB8606 (provided by Francis Barr’s laboratory53), using the following primers: P2A-CT linker forward 5′-ggtgggagcggtggtggcg-3′ and P2A-CT Neo reverse 5′-tcagaagaactcgtcaagaaggcgat-3′.

All PCR products were run on 0.8% TBE agarose gels, and bands were gel extracted and assembled into EcoRV-digested pSKII(-) using Gibson assembly with a NEB HiFi Assembly kit, according to the manufacturer’s instructions for four-way assembly. The final homology donor plasmid and the pX459 plasmid containing the gRNA were sequenced using Sanger sequencing to confirm the correct sequence.

Generation of CDC25A–GFP-tagged cell line. hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cells56 in a six-well plate (Sarstedt, 83.3920) were forward transfected with Lipofectamine 2000 and 500 ng of pX459-gRNA and 500 ng of pSK-CDC25A-CT-GFP, according to the manufacturer’s protocol, and left to recover overnight. The next day, cells were split into 15-cm tissue culture dishes (Corning, 420599) and, 24 h later, clones were selected with 1 mg ml−1 G418. Media and antibiotic were refreshed every 3–4 days. After 2 weeks, single colonies were selected using cloning cylinders (Sigma) and transferred to 24-well plates. Cells were expanded and initially screened for GFP positivity in the nucleus. Genomic DNA was extracted from cells with GFP-positive nuclei, using a Qiagen DNeasy blood and tissue kit, according to the manufacturer’s instructions. To check for targeted integration of the C-terminal tagging construct, the following primers were used: 5′-atgtacagtcgtctgaag and 5′-taccacagcgcctcctgg. All PCR products were sequenced by Sanger sequencing to check for in-frame tagging. Clones were then further validated by western blotting, growth curves and single-cell imaging to check cell cycle phase lengths. We obtained several heterozygously tagged clones that had a slightly decreased proliferation rate, mainly owing to an increase in G1 length (Extended Data Fig. 6b–e). CDC25A–GFP localized correctly to the nucleus (Extended Data Fig. 6f). In unperturbed cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, CDC25A–GFP showed accumulation around the G1/S transition, as previously described57.

For endogenous expression of the PCNA reporter, the sequence encoding fluorescent protein mRuby was inserted in the first exon of one allele of the PCNA locus by recombinant adeno-associated virus (rAAV)-mediated homologous recombination56 in the hTert-RPE1 MYC-OsTir1 WEE1–GFP–AID–Neo cell line. Then, 293T cells were seeded in a T-75 flask so that they reached 50–70% confluence at the time of transfection. pAAV–mRuby–PCNA (3 μg; gift from Joerg Mansfeld, ICR56) and pRC and pHelper plasmids (9 μg total) were mixed with 3.75 ml of Opti-MEM (Invitrogen), after which 19 μl of PLUS Reagent (Invitrogen) was added, and the mixture was incubated for 5–10 min at room temperature. Next, 47 μl of Lipofectamine LTX (Invitrogen) was added to the tube, followed by incubation for 30 min. 15 minutes before the end of the incubation, 293T cells were washed twice with PBS; then, 7.5 ml of Opti-MEM (Invitrogen) was added, and the cells were incubated at 37 °C for 15 min. After this, 1.5 ml of DNA/lipid mixture was added to the cells, followed by incubation at 37 °C for 4–6 h, and then the medium was replaced with complete DMEM. 2–3 days postinfection, cells in medium (and any floating cells) were transferred to a sterile 50-ml Falcon tube. Then, 5 ml of fresh medium was added to the flask, and adherent cells were scraped off and transferred to the same 50-ml Falcon tube. Cells were lysed with three rounds of freezing and thawing (10 min in dry ice and ethanol + 10 min in a water bath at 37 °C). After each cycle, cells were vortexed at full speed for 1 min. Cells were then centrifuged at 10,000g for 30 min at 4 °C. Tubes were wiped with ethanol and placed in the tissue culture hood. Cell-free supernatants were collected, and rAAV preparations were dispensed in small aliquots (3–5 ml). hTert-RPE1 MYC-OsTir1 WEE1–GFP–AID–Neo cells were seeded in a T-75 flask so that they reached 30–40% confluence at the time of infection. The T-75 flask was washed twice with PBS; then, 5 ml of complete medium and 5 ml of rAAV stock were added, and the flask was left to incubate at 37 °C for 4 h. Fresh media were added to bring the volume up to 15 ml (or more), and infection was allowed to continue for 48 h. After 3 days, mRuby–PCNA-positive cells were sorted to single-cell density in 50:50 conditioned/growth media in 96-well plates by fluorescence-activated cell sorting (FACS). Cells were left to expand for 2 weeks, and mRuby–PCNA-positive clones were identified by microscopy.

p21-knockout MCF7, KU1919 and HBLAK cell lines were generated as previously described34. The only modification was that HBLAK cells were transfected by nucleofection on the Amaxa nucleofector, using the MCF7 protocol. Knockouts were validated by immunostaining and western blotting for p21 after Nutlin-3 treatment, to boost the p21 signal.

All siRNAs used are listed in Supplementary Table 4. For live imaging and western blotting experiments, cells were reverse transfected with siRNA in 384-well PhenoPlates (Revvity) and 24-well plates (Greiner Bio-One, 662165), respectively. For imaging experiments, transfection was carried out 24 h after CDK4/6 inhibition in a total volume of 30 µl of medium and used at a final concentration of 20 nM. Transfection was performed using Lipofectamine RNAiMAX (Invitrogen 13778150), according to the manufacturer’s instructions. In brief, 40 nl of Lipofectamine RNAiMAX was added to 40 nl of 20 µM siRNA in 10 µl Opti-MEM (Gibco 31985062), and 10 µl of the mixture was subsequently added to cells. Following transfection, cells were incubated for 6–24 h, depending on the experiment. For western blotting, this protocol was carried out with the following differences: cells were grown in 500 µl of medium; and 1 µl of Lipofectamine RNAiMAX was added to 1 µl of 20 µM siRNA in 100 µl Opti-MEM, and the mixture was subsequently added to cells. Following transfection, cells were incubated for 6–24 h, depending on the experiment.

All antibodies used for immunostaining are listed in Supplementary Table 5. Cells were grown in 96- or 384-well PhenoPlates (Revvity, 6055302 and 6057302, respectively). EdU was added to a final concentration of 10 μM for either 12–24h at G0 release or with a 30-min pulse before fixation for identification of G0 and S phase cells, respectively. Cells were fixed in 4% formaldehyde–PBS for 15 min at room temperature, permeabilized with PBS–0.5% Triton X-100 for 15 min and blocked for 1 h in blocking buffer (PBS with 2% bovine serum albumin (BSA), filtered). Primary antibodies were diluted in blocking buffer and incubated overnight at 4 °C or for 2 h at room temperature. After three washes in PBS, cells were incubated for 1 h at room temperature in the dark with conjugated secondary antibodies (1:1,000; Invitrogen). For EdU incorporation, cells were incubated with 100 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.5, 4 mM CuSO4, 100 mM ascorbic acid and 5 μM sulfo-cyanine-5 azide for 30 min at room temperature in the dark, washed three times in PBS and counterstained with 1 μg ml−1 Hoechst for 10 min, followed by three washes in PBS. All plate-washing steps were performed on an automated 50TS microplate washer (BioTek). Plates were imaged using an Operetta CLS (Revvity) with a ×20 (numerical aperture (NA) 0.8) objective.

All automated image analysis of images acquired using Operetta CLS was performed using Harmony software (Revvity). Nuclei were segmented on the basis of Hoechst intensity, with nuclei at the edge of the field of view excluded from the analysis. The nuclear intensity of proteins was calculated as the mean intensity in the segmented region.

G0 cells were identified by the absence of EdU staining. Briefly, the nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio was calculated by creation of a 4-pixel-width ring around the segmented nucleus, and the ring intensity was calculated as a proxy for the cytoplasmic portion. Nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratios for individual cells were calculated and plotted in Prism (GraphPad). Cells with EdU nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratios less than 1.2 were called as G0.

All antibodies used for western blotting are listed in Supplementary Table 4.

Whole-cell lysates were prepared following aspiration of medium from culture plates, followed by washing with PBS on ice. Lysates were collected in 1× Novex Tris-Glycine SDS sample buffer (Novex, LC2676) supplemented with 1× phosphatase inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 1862495), 1× protease inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 78429) and 1 mM dithiothreitol (DTT; BioUltra, 43816-10ML). Samples were incubated at 95 °C for 10 min, then centrifuged at 14,000g for 1 min before being loaded on a Novex 4–20% Tris-Glycine gel (Invitrogen, XP04205). Proteins were transferred to PVDF-FL membranes (Merck Life Sciences, IPFL00010), which were then blocked in blocking buffer (Tris-buffered saline (TBS), 5% milk, 10% glycerol, Tween 0.1%) for 1 h at room temperature and incubated with primary antibodies diluted in blocking buffer, overnight at 4 °C. Membranes were washed three times in TBS with 0.05% Triton X-100, then incubated for 1 h at room temperature with HRP-conjugated secondary antibodies diluted in blocking buffer. Membranes were then washed three times in TBS with 0.05% Triton X-100 and developed using Clarity Western ECL Substrate (Bio-Rad, 1705061) unless secondary antibodies were conjugated to Alexa Fluor. Blots were imaged on an Amersham Imager 680.

For live cell imaging involving WEE1 degradation with doxycycline and IAA, RPE1 WEE1–GFP–AID mRuby–PCNA cells were plated in 384-well PhenoPlates (Revvity) at a density of 500 cells per well in 20 µl medium. Cells were synchronized with 1 µM CDK4/6i for 24 h, and doxycycline and IAA (doxycycline: 1 µg ml−1; IAA: 500 µM) was added to the growth medium when cells were released. For western blots, doxycycline or IAA was also added at release.

All purified proteins used for in vitro kinase assays are listed in Supplementary Table 6.

To confirm the activity of the WEE1 kinase, we carried out a positive control experiment with cyclin B1–CDK1. The in vitro kinase protocol was provided by J. Pines (ICR). For this reaction, 100 ng of recombinant cyclin B1–CDK1 (Abcam) was diluted in 5 µl of kinase buffer (200 mM Tris pH 7.5, 150 mM NaCl, 10 mM MgCl2, 1 mM DTT) with 0.5 mM ATP and 18 µM RO-3306 (CDK1 inhibitor, Selleckchem). This mixture was added to 50 ng of recombinant WEE1 (Thermo Fisher Scientific) diluted in 5 µl of kinase buffer with 0.5 µM MK-1775 (WEE1i, Selleckchem) for the negative control. A negative control without addition of WEE1 kinase was used. The reactions were incubated for 30 min at 30 °C, stopped by addition of 5 µl of 3× SDS buffer with 100 mM DTT and boiled at 95 °C for 5 min. Samples were then used for western blotting as described, with the exception that milk was replaced with BSA at the blocking step. pY15-CDK1/2/3/5 and CDK1 antibodies were used to quantify pY15-CDK1 phosphorylation. For monitoring of phosphorylation of CDH1 on tyrosine residues by WEE1, 80 ng of recombinant Strep–CDH1 was diluted in 5 µl of kinase buffer with 0.5 mM ATP. This mixture was added to 50 ng of recombinant WEE1 diluted in 5 µl of kinase buffer with 0.5 µM MK-1775 for the negative control. A negative control without kinase addition was also carried out. The reactions were incubated for 30 min at 30 °C, stopped by addition of 5 µl of 3× SDS buffer with 100 mM DTT and boiled at 95 °C for 5 min. As for the cyclin B1–CDK1 reaction, samples were analysed by western blotting. The phospho-tyrosine antibody pTyr-100 and CDH1 antibody were used to quantify phosphorylation of tyrosine residues on CDH1. For the results shown in Extended Data Fig. 7g, 1.5 µg of bacterially expressed and purified Xenopus GST-FZR N186 (Cdh1) or GST-FZY N159 (Cdc20) protein58 was incubated with 160 ng recombinant WEE1 kinase (Thermo Fisher Scientific) in 10 µl kinase buffer. Samples were collected at the indicated time points and analysed by SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting using an anti-phospho-tyrosine antibody (pTyr-100). For the results shown in Extended Data Fig. 7j, bacterially expressed and purified human GST–CDH1 N186 or its tyrosine variants were incubated with recombinant WEE1 kinase in the same kinase buffer for 10 min and analysed by SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting using the same anti-phospho-tyrosine antibody.

For monitoring of CDC6–mVenus stability upon WEE1 inhibition, hTert-RPE1 CDC6–mVenus DHB–mCherry mTurq2–PCNA cells were plated in a 24-well plate at a density of 50,000 cells per well. Cells were arrested with CDK4/6i for 24 h and treated with WEE1i (1 µM) or DMSO upon release. 2 h after release, cells were treated with cycloheximide (100 µg ml−1) every 30 min for 2 h. Subsequently, cells were lysed in 1× Novex Tris-Glycine SDS sample buffer (Novex, LC2676) supplemented with 1× phosphatase inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 1862495), 1× protease inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 78429) and 1 mM DTT (BioUltra, 43816-10ML).

For monitoring of CDC6–mVenus and CDC25A protein levels upon WEE1 and proteasome inhibition, hTert-RPE1 CDC6–mVenus DHB–mCherry mTurq2–PCNA cells were plated in a 24-well plate at a density of 50,000 cells per well, or hTert-RPE1 cells were plated in a 6-well plate at a density of 300,000 cells per well. Cells were arrested with CDK4/6i for 24 h and treated with WEE1i (1 µM) or DMSO upon release. 2 h after release, cells were treated with proteasome inhibitor MG132 (10 µM) for 2 h. Subsequently, cells were lysed in 1× Novex Tris-Glycine SDS sample buffer (Novex, LC2676) supplemented with 1× phosphatase inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 1862495), 1× protease inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 78429) and 1 mM DTT (BioUltra, 43816-10ML).

For monitoring of CDC25A transcription levels, hTert-RPE1 cells were plated in a 96-well plate at a density of 5,000 cells per well. Cells were arrested with CDK4/6i for 24 h and treated with WEE1i (1 µM) or DMSO upon release. 4 h after release, cells were fixed in 4% formaldehyde in PBS for 15 min at room temperature. Cells were then processed with a ViewRNA-ISH cell assay kit (Thermo Scientific, QVC0001). In brief, cells were permeabilized with proteases; then, the CDC25A probe (VA6-3168938 ViewRNA Type 6 probe set CDC25A, Thermo Scientific) was hybridized for 3 h at 40 °C. Subsequently, the preamplifier was hybridized for 30 min at 40 °C, followed by hybridization with the amplifier for 30 min at 40 °C and finally hybridization with the label probe for 30 min at 40 °C. Nuclei were stained with DAPI, and the plate was imaged using an Operetta CLS (Revvity) with a ×40 water (NA 1.1) objective. The cytoplasm was imaged with the digital phase contrast channel. Cytoplasmic segmentation was performed with Harmony software (Revvity) using Find Cytoplasm method D, and cellular CDC25A mRNA foci were detected with Find Spots method A.

Expression and purification of Strep–CDH1 was performed using a method similar to that described in ref. 59, with some modifications. Specifically, codon-optimized complementary DNAs of Strep–CDH1 in pFastBac1 vectors were ordered from GeneArt (Thermo Fisher Scientific) and transformed into MultiBac cells for expression in insect cells. The baculovirus was created in Sf9 cells; this virus was then used to infect Hi5 cells at a cell density of 1 × 106 cells ml−1, followed by incubation for 72 h at 27 °C, 130 rpm. All purification steps were carried out at 4 °C. First, the cell pellet was thawed on ice in wash buffer (50 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.3, 500 mM NaCl, 5% glycerol, 0.5 mM TCE)] supplemented with 5 units ml−1 benzonase and an EDTA-free protease inhibitor (Roche). The cells were sonicated and then centrifuged for 1 h at 48,000g. The supernatant was then bound to a 5-ml StrepTactin Superflow Plus cartridge (Qiagen) using a flow rate of 1 ml min−1. The column was washed extensively with wash buffer and then eluted using elution buffer (50 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.0, 200 mM NaCl, 5% glycerol, 0.5 mM TCEP and 2.5 mM desthiobiotin).

Hs CDH1 WT or tyrosine mutant proteins (Y91E, Y130E, Y91E/Y130E, or Y91E/T129D/Y130E), prepared using a coupled in vitro transcription–translation system (TnT, Promega), were mixed with Xenopus egg interphase extracts for 30 min at 23 °C. Endogenous APC/C was then immunoprecipitated using Dynabeads crosslinked to the AF3.1 anti-APC3 antibody. The immobilized APC/C was washed twice with 1× XBcsf-MS (10 mM HEPES-KOH pH 7.8, 50 mM sucrose, 300 mM KCl, 2 mM MgCl2 and 5 mM EGTA) containing 0.01% NP-40, eluted in 1× SDS sample buffer and analysed by SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting.

Destruction assays were performed essentially as described previously60. APC/C substrates were labelled with [35S]methionine/cysteine (Hartmann Analytic) using TnT (Promega, UK). CDH1-mediated destruction assays were carried out in Xenopus egg cell-free interphase extracts supplemented with Hs CDH1 WT or tyrosine mutant proteins prepared by TnT. Full-length cyclin B and cyclin B Δ67, which lacks the D-box, served as positive and negative controls, respectively. Samples were taken at the indicated time points and analysed by SDS–PAGE and autoradiography.

To determine the tyrosine residues phosphorylated by WEE1 on CDH1, the reactions (5 replicates per condition) were carried out as described in the ‘In vitro kinase assays’ section, with the exception that the reactions were stopped with addition of 1 M guanidine hydrochloride. We diluted 5 μl of the kinase reaction with 45 μl water containing TCEP (final concentration 10 mM) and chloracetamide (20 mM). The reaction was incubated at 37 °C to reduce and alkylate Cys, and digestion was initiated by addition of trypsin and LysC at final concentrations of 5 ng µl−1 and 10 ng µl−1, respectively. After overnight digestion the samples were desalted on 3 mg of oasis HLB buffer. The samples were evaporated in a SpeedVac and resuspended in 30 µl of 0.1% trifluoroacetic acid, and 5 µl was analysed using liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry.

Chromatographic separation was performed using an Ultimate 3000 RSLC nano liquid chromatography system coupled to a Exploris 240 mass spectrometer with an EASY-Spray source. Electrospray ionization was achieved with Bruker PepSep emitters (PN: PSFSELJ10, 10 µm). Peptide solutions were injected directly on to the analytical column (self-packed column, CSH C18 1.7-µm beads, 150 μm × 30 cm) at a working flow rate of 1.25 μl min−1 for 4 min. Peptides were then separated using a 60-min stepped gradient: 0–45% of buffer B for 70 min (composition of buffer A: 95%:5% H2O/DMSO + 0.1% formic acid; composition of buffer B: 75%:20%:5% MeCN/H2O/DMSO + 0.1% formic acid), followed by column conditioning and equilibration. Eluted peptides were analysed in data-independent acquisition (DIA) mode: an initial MS1 scan was carried out at 140,000 resolution with an AGC target of 3e6 for a maximum IT of 200 ms, m/z range: 410–1,600. This was followed by 30 MS2 scans at 30 K resolution covering 410–1,600 with variable window sizes predicted by encyclopeDIA. The AGC target was set to 3e6 with maximum IT on auto. Normalized collision energy was set to 27%.

Data were processed using the Spectronaut software platform (Biognosys, 19.9.250512.62635) in direct DIA mode. Searches were carried out against Spodoptera frugiperda concatenated with the human WEE1 sequence, the Trichoplusia ni protein sequence database concatenated with the human CDH1 sequence and a universal protein contaminants database61. For the DIA analysis, higher stringency settings were used than in the default workflow as described previously62. Default settings were used for posttranslational modification analysis, with the probability cutoff for posttranslational modification localization set to 0, before table export. To account for CDH1 intensity variance at the protein level, phospho-site intensities were also normalized to the total protein level. Specific sites of interest were validated using the Spectronaut feature viewer.

The two identified phospho-sites were located on the DGLAY[phospho (Y)]SALLK–Y91 and GLFTY[phospho (Y)]SLSTK–Y130 peptides, both with 2+ charge. We reran the samples targeting the peptides in parallel reaction monitoring and single ion monitoring modes at 240 K resolution and 500 ms maximum injection time. The mass spectrometry proteomics data have been deposited at the ProteomeXchange Consortium through the PRIDE63 partner repository with dataset identifier PXD068577.

FACS of cells was performed at the Flow Cytometry Facility, MRC Laboratory of Medical Sciences (MRC LMS). Cells were washed with PBS and resuspended in FACS sorting buffer (1 ml of PBS, 2% FBS) to a concentration of 8–12 million cells ml−1. Before sorting, cells were filtered through a 30- to 40-μM filter.

Cells were plated at a density of 1,000 cells per well in triplicate in 96-well tissue culture plates. Bright-field images were taken every 4 h for 7 days using a ×10 (NA 0.3) objective, and percentage confluency was calculated on a CellCyteX (Echo) live imaging system. For calculation of confluency, a mask was created using the default settings on the CellCyteX (brightness 0%, contrast 0%, contrast sensitivity 50 a.u., smoothing 2 a.u. and filled hole size 100 µm2). Results were plotted with Python (Seaborn package).

Cells were plated at a density of 500 cells per well in 20 µl of medium. For the drug dose curve assays with siRNA transfection, cells were transfected (10 µl) for 24 h before drug addition as described previously. MK-1775 was prepared by 7 × 1:3 serial dilutions (7 × 1:4 in the case of transfection) in growth medium with final concentrations for all assays of 100 µM, 10 µM, 1 µM, 0.1 µM, 0.01 µM and 0.001 µM. Ten microlitres were added per well. After 72 h of incubation, cells were fixed and stained for Hoechst. Plates were imaged using an Operetta CLS (Revvity) with a ×20 (NA 0.8) objective. Cell viability was calculated as a percentage, and normalized half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values were computed using GraphPad Prism.

hTert-RPE1 CCNB1–eYFP mTurq2–EMI1 53BP1-mCherry cells (a gift from R. Medema64) were seeded in a 384-well PhenoPlate (Revvity) at a density of 500 cells per well in 20 µl of medium. Cells were arrested for 24 h in CDK4/6i and then released with doxycycline (1 µg ml−1) to induce EMI1 expression, EdU, and DMSO or WEE1i for 12 h before fixation for identification of G0 cells. Cells were fixed in 4% formaldehyde–PBS for 15 min at room temperature, permeabilized with PBS–0.5% Triton X-100 for 15 min and blocked for 1 h in blocking buffer (PBS–2% BSA, filtered). For EdU incorporation, cells were incubated with 100 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.5, 4 mM CuSO4, 100 mM ascorbic acid and 5 μM sulfo-cyanine-5 azide for 30 min at room temperature in the dark, washed three times in PBS and counterstained with 1 μg ml−1 Hoechst for 10 min, followed by three washes in PBS. Inhibition of CDH1 was confirmed by western blot. Cells were plated at a density of 40,000 cells per well in a 24-well plate, then arrested for 24 h with CDK4/6i and released with addition of doxycycline to induce EMI1 expression (1 µg ml−1 and 10 µg ml−1) for 4 h before cell lysis in 1× Novex Tris-Glycine SDS sample buffer (Novex, LC2676) supplemented with 1× phosphatase inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 1862495), 1× protease inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 78429) and 1 mM DTT (BioUltra, 43816-10ML).

Live cell imaging was performed on an Operetta CLS High-Content Analysis microscope (Revvity), with atmospheric control to maintain cells at 37 °C and 5% CO2 and a breathable membrane (Thermo Fisher Scientific) to stop evaporation. Cells were plated in a 384-well PhenoPlate (Revvity) at a density of 500 cells per well in 20 μl of medium for 24 h (unless otherwise stated) before imaging. Cells were imaged using a ×20 (NA 0.8) objective at 10-min intervals for 48 h (unless otherwise stated) in 50 μl phenol red-free media. For hTert-RPE1 WEE1–GFP–AID mRuby–PCNA and hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA CDC25A–GFP live imaging, cells were imaged on an automated inverted spinning disc confocal microscope system IX83 (Olympus) combined with CellVivo (Olympus). Cells were plated on IbiTreat μ-slide 8-well (ibidi) plates at a density of 5000 cells per well in 200 μl of medium or in a 384-well PhenoPlate (Revvity) at a density of 500 cells per well in 20 μl of medium, 24 h before imaging (unless otherwise stated). Images were typically acquired with a ×20 plan (UCPLFLN) fluorescence objective (NA 0.7).

Images were exported as tiff files and analysed using NucliTrack65 and/or Fiji and Napari66. All cells analysed were present throughout the entire movie unless they died. Spontaneous death was quantified manually. mRuby–PCNA was used to define cell cycle phases34,56. We defined S phase entry as the time point at which the mRuby–PCNA mean intensity started to increase and mRuby–PCNA switched from being homogenously localized throughout the nucleus to having a more textured appearance; PCNA binds to the chromatin at the start of S phase, which can be measured by an increase in the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA intensity. Example images around the G1/S transition and the output of mRuby–PCNA features around the G1/S transition from Nuclitrack are shown in Extended Data Fig. 1e.

As shown in Fig. 3b, the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA was used to quantify progression through S phase. Cells in G1 had a low standard deviation, as the mRuby–PCNA intensity was homogenous throughout the nucleus. As cells progressed through S phase, the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA intensity increased as many small foci coalesced into larger, brighter foci, leading to very dark and very bright areas of the nucleus.

The code used for single-cell time-lapse image segmentation and tracking was implemented in Python (Jupyter notebook) and is available at GitHub (https://github.com/Alexis-Barr-Lab). This analysis pipeline combined Cellpose67 segmentation and tracking using Ultrack68 and was run using the Jex HPC cluster at the MRC LMS69. In brief, raw TIFF stacks (shape: T × Y × X) were background-subtracted using a rolling ball-style method with a Gaussian filter (σ = 15 px), followed by morphological reconstruction to separate foreground from low-frequency background. Frames were normalized (γ = 0.5) and stored in Zarr format for efficient chunked input–output. Normalized frames were processed with a Cellpose model (cpsam). Masks for each time point were generated, yielding per-frame two-dimensional label arrays written into a Zarr volume. A watershed was run on each frame to recover fine cell boundaries and split merged objects. Thresholding and binary morphology mask detections, followed by distance transform watershed with minima suppression, were performed to generate second label sets, which were also saved as Zarr. Graphics processing unit-accelerated boundary detection was implemented in CuPy; boundaries from both Cellpose and watershed masks were combined and smoothed (σ = 5) to create high-resolution contour maps for downstream linking. Centroids of watershed segments between frames were calculated. The Hungarian algorithm (linear_sum_assignment) was used to link cells, providing centroid-distance statistics (95th percentile, maximum, average and 25th percentile displacements) and informing Ultrack’s max_distance parameter. Ultrack was then configured and executed. For quality checks, tracks and labels were exported and visualized. Background subtraction was then applied to the channel of interest, and properties were exported.

Statistical analyses in this work were performed in GraphPad Prism unless otherwise stated.

For generation of MCF7 WEE1i-resistant cells, MCF7 WT cells were plated on a 15-cm tissue culture dish (Corning, 420599) at a density of 5 × 106 cells ml−1. Cells were treated with 0.075 µM MK-1775 24 h later and incubated until they reached confluence. Cells were then split and treated with a double amount of MK-1775 24 h later and incubated again until confluence. This step was repeated until 0.6 µM MK-1775 was reached. The resistance of these cells was measured against the parental cells with a 72-h drug dose curve (IC50).

MCF7-resistant and WT cells were plated at a density of 100,000 cells per well in a 6-well plate (Sarstedt, 83.3920). Twenty-four hours later, total RNA was extracted using an RNeasy MinElute Cleanup Kit (Qiagen 74204) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Quality and concentration were assessed using the Agilent 2100 Bioanalyzer RNA 6000 Nano assay.

RNA sequencing libraries were constructed from 200 ng of total RNA using the Watchmaker Genomics mRNA enrichment and RNA Library Prep kits according to the manufacturer’s instructions. Libraries were dual indexed using IDT xGen Unique Dual Index Primers, and library quality was assessed by Agilent 2100 Bioanalyzer High-Sensitivity DNA assay. Libraries were pooled and run on an Illumina NextSeq 2000, generating a minimum of 60 million paired-end 60-bp reads per sample.

RNA sequencing reads were processed using cutadapt v.4.7 (ref. 70) to remove Illumina adapters, to quality trim at Q20, and to filter read pairs that contained N bases or in which either read was less than 31 bp. Processed reads were quantified with Salmon v.1.9.0 (ref. 71) using transcripts from the GRCh38 Ensembl v.107 annotations. Salmon’s expectation maximization procedures were set to enable modelling of sequencing and GC biases.

In R, transcript level counts from Salmon were imported and aggregated to the gene level using tximport v.1.32.0 (ref. 72). Sample quality assessments were performed in relation to collected RNA integrity values, sample concentrations used in library preparation (ng μl−1), read GC content and percentage read mapping. Normalization, further quality control based on principal component analysis and clustering using normalized values, and differential expression analyses were performed with DESeq2 v.1.44.0 (ref. 70). We conducted independent hypothesis weighting to optimize the power of P value filtering73 using IHW v.1.32.0 and log2 fold change shrinkage using ashr v.2.2-63 (ref. 74) to reduce the impact of low expression on estimation of fold change values. Multiple correction testing adjustments were optimized using thresholds for false discovery rate and log2 fold change at the point of generation of results in DESeq2. Significance was assessed using a q value of 0.01.

To monitor CDH1 stability upon WEE1 inhibition, we plated RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cells in a 24-well plate at a density of 50,000 cells per well. Cells were arrested with CDK4/6i for 24 h and treated with WEE1i (1 µM) or DMSO and cycloheximide (100 µg ml−1) upon release. Subsequently, cells were lysed every hour for 4 h in 1× Novex Tris-Glycine SDS sample buffer (Novex, LC2676) supplemented with 1× phosphatase inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 1862495), 1× protease inhibitors (Thermo Scientific, 78429) and 1 mM DTT (BioUltra, 43816-10ML).

APC/C activity (kAPC) calculations based on mCherry–geminin levels (APC degron), expressed in the PIP-FUCCI hTert-RPE1 cell line75 (a gift from J. Cook, University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill), were calculated as previously described21,76. Specifically, we assumed degron dynamics were described by differential equation (1):

where ksynth is the constitutive rate of APC degron synthesis and kAPC is the APC activity. The equation can be rearranged for kAPC:

Thus, we were able to use the fluorescence signal of our degron cell lines to estimate each term in equation (2).

First, we aligned our fluorescence intensity time series data from each cell to the G1/S transition point. Subsequently, we averaged the signal across cells at each time point and smoothed the data by calculating the three-point running mean. We then subtracted the background signal from the resulting value at each time point. The resulting series was a measure of degron concentration, corresponding to [APC degron], in arbitrary units. The derivative term was calculated by applying the finite difference method to the consecutive points of the [APC degron] time series. ksynth was estimated from the initial (net) rate of degron accumulation in S phase, when APC/C was assumed to be inactive.

Clustal Omega77 was used to align CDH1 protein sequences, and outputs (consensus frequency of residues, identity matrix and phylogenetic tree) were plotted in Python.

We built our cell cycle model using a system of coupled nonlinear differential equations describing the rates of change of biochemical species responsible for regulation of the G1 and S states. The regulation of subsequent cell cycle phases was omitted for simplicity, as our experimental approach was focused on quiescence release rather than cycling cells. A description of the modelling and the parameters used are available in the Supplementary Information.

Further information on research design is available in the Nature Portfolio Reporting Summary linked to this article.

All RNA sequencing data generated in this study are publicly available from the Gene Expression Omnibus under accession GSE302212. The mass spectrometry proteomics data have been deposited at the ProteomeXchange Consortium through the PRIDE partner repository with dataset identifier PXD068577. Source data are provided with this paper.

The code used for single-cell time-lapse image segmentation and tracking was implemented in Python (Jupyter notebook) and is available at GitHub (https://github.com/Alexis-Barr-Lab).

Output from “CDK2-like” ODE model for WEE1 inhibition of CDH1 for wild-type WEE1 activity (a), 50% of WEE1 activity (b) and 0% WEE1 activity (c). d. G1 length plotted as a function of WEE1 activity showing an infinite G1 length (G0 arrest) at very low levels of WEE1 activity. In the “CDK2-like” model, CDH1 hyper-phosphorylation and inactivation are determined by a simple (weighted) sum of CyclinE/CDK2, CyclinA/CDK2 and WEE1 activities. This model predicts that WEE1 downregulation to 50% of wild-type activity advances CyclinE/CDK2 activation, but also that CyclinE must accumulate to a much higher level to compensate for WEE1 depletion, leading to delayed CDH1 inactivation and G1/S entry. Output from “threshold” ODE model for WEE1 inhibition of CDH1 for wild-type WEE1 activity (e), 50% of WEE1 activity (f) and 0% WEE1 activity (g). h. G1 length plotted as a function of WEE1 activity showing an infinite G1 length (G0 arrest) at very low levels of WEE1 activity. In the “threshold” model, CyclinE/CDK2 activation is also advanced. One distinct feature of this model is that APC/CCdh1 inactivation dynamics display a two-phase profile upon WEE1 inhibition. First, CDH1 is phosphorylated gradually by the accumulating CyclinE/CDK2 and second, the remaining CDH1 is rapidly inactivated once CyclinA starts to accumulate. Output from “independent” ODE model for WEE1 inhibition of CDH1 for wild-type WEE1 activity (i), 50% of WEE1 activity (j) and 0% WEE1 activity (k). l. G1 length plotted as a function of WEE1 activity showing an infinite G1 length (G0 arrest) at very low levels of WEE1 activity. Notably, in this instance, partial WEE1 inactivation results in delayed G1/S entry and greater APC/CCdh1 activity in G1. Nevertheless, APC/CCdh1 is otherwise inactivated in a sharp, stepwise manner at G1/S under wild-type and 50% WEE1 activity conditions alike.

#2033. AI legal research tools offer small firms more choices

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For many years, Westlaw and LexisNexis dominated the online legal research market for law firms of all sizes. Lawyers from firms big and small seeking to conduct research had very few options: Go the old-school route and use books, pay high fees, or obtain free access at their local law libraries.

Then, right before the turn of the century, things changed. A handful of online legal research alternatives became available that were much more affordable for solo and small-firm lawyers. The most popular platforms were notably Fastcase and Casemaker. Even better, both of these companies began to establish partnerships with bar associations across the country, giving most members free access to the legal research platforms, bridging the gap for solo and small firms. Casetext’s arrival in 2013 gave solos and small firms another affordable option.

Nearly a decade later, however, the legal research landscape began to change. In 2021, Fastcase and Casemaker merged, reducing the number of independent competitors offering affordable research options for smaller firms. Then, in 2023, Fastcase merged with vLex, a global legal research company now owned by Clio (also an advertiser with the ABA Journal), and Casetext was acquired by Thomson Reuters.

Even as these acquisitions and mergers occurred, new artificial intelligence-based research startups emerged around that same time, providing small firms with stand-alone, cost-effective AI-powered legal research tools. For example, Midpage was founded in 2022, and Descrybe launched in 2023. We’ll discuss both of those tools below, along with two more recent entrants into the market, all of which are priced at $100 or less per month—far lower than the average monthly costs of tools targeted at the larger-firm market.

There are a number of factors to address before considering which tools to add to your firm’s tech stack. For example, does the tool’s coverage include the caselaw and statutory databases your firm requires? If they’re not included in the base price, how much more will it cost to access them? Are there any other additional costs you should be aware of?

Another question to consider is whether you have complimentary access through a bar association membership. For example, Fastcase, without vLex’s AI features included, continues to be available for free through some bar association memberships. Other bar associations have migrated to another legal research tool, Decisis. If you have access to either tool through your membership, you’ll want to determine which databases are covered under the complimentary plan—some free plans will only include access to your state law database—and whether they’re sufficient for your firm’s needs.

Next, for any AI-powered tools, you’ll need to understand the hallucination rate and any features that have been added to assist with confirming the accuracy of results. Some research platforms include citators that identify subsequent treatment of cases and help lawyers determine whether an authority remains good law, while others offer tools that verify the accuracy of case citations. There should also be features available that allow you to quickly and easily verify AI output, whether it’s a link that you can click on that takes you to the source material or the ability to view the source material in a sidebar on your computer’s screen.

Another factor to consider is whether the benefits of using AI for legal research outweigh the costs, both in terms of time spent verifying the output and the money spent on the tools. Carefully consider your current workflows, needs and existing technology before you commit to vetting AI legal research products. It’s entirely possible that you may find that the traditional legal research platforms already in place in your firm are more economical than adding yet another costly subscription to your firm’s technology budget.

If you conclude that moving forward makes sense, the next step is to carefully evaluate the available options. As with any cloud-based legal software, choosing a provider requires more than comparing features and pricing. You have an ethical obligation of technology competence, which means you must take steps to understand how the technology provider handles confidential information, including where data is stored, who can access it, what security measures are in place, and how often backups occur.

AI-powered tools raise additional questions that should be addressed before making a purchasing decision. For example, how reliable are the citation verification results, and what steps has the company taken to measure accuracy? Will information uploaded by your firm be used to train AI models? What protections are in place for confidential client data? And which legal research databases does the software access when checking citations? The answers can make a significant difference in the usefulness of the tool, its cost and the risks associated with its adoption.

With those considerations in mind, let’s take a look at some of the more affordable AI-powered legal research tools currently available that are designed for small-firm practitioners. Note that this isn’t an exhaustive list, and we’ll focus only on stand-alone legal research products with published subscription costs of $100 or less per user per month.

First, there’s Descrybe, an AI-powered legal research platform that provides access to U.S. caselaw and other primary legal authorities using natural-language searches. Its features include case summaries; legal issue analysis; and an AI-powered citator called Cytator, which analyzes how courts have treated legal authorities. The cost is $50 per user per month for the full research platform, which includes Legal Engine access, or $25 per user per month for its Legal Engine alone, which integrates with AI assistants including ChatGPT and Claude. Subscriptions to those assistants must be purchased separately.

Another option is Midpage, an AI-powered legal research platform that allows lawyers to search federal and state caselaw and statutes via natural-language queries. It includes agentic research capabilities that investigate legal questions across multiple sources, along with an AI citator for checking the subsequent treatment of cases. Midpage is also available as a plugin for Claude, ChatGPT and Perplexity. The cost is $100 per user per month or $80 per user per month with annual billing.

Next is Cases, an AI-powered tool designed for solo practitioners and small firms that focuses on searching federal and state caselaw using plain-English questions. Search results include relevant judicial decisions, summaries of holdings and citations to the underlying opinions, with higher-tier plans offering research history and good-law flags—which identify cases that warrant reverification. Pricing starts at $24 per user per month or $288 per user if billed annually.

Finally, CaseSearch is an AI-powered legal research platform that enables lawyers to search federal and state caselaw using natural-language questions and locate relevant statutory authorities. It provides case summaries, citations and treatment checks to help users evaluate whether decisions remain good law. Monthly pricing is $49 per user per month, or it’s $24 per user per month with annual billing.

The growing number of AI-powered legal research startups is good news for solo and small-firm lawyers. Increased competition means more affordable options along with access to increasingly sophisticated research capabilities. These newer platforms are expanding beyond traditional legal research features, offering AI-powered case analysis, citation verification and more.

As this category continues to evolve, smaller firms will have more opportunities to access advanced legal research technologies. Of course, in the AI era, careful vetting remains essential, but the expanding market offers promising alternatives for small firms without limitless IT budgets that are seeking affordable and powerful legal research options.

Nicole Black is a Rochester, New York-based attorney, author and journalist. She is the principal legal insight strategist at 8am, parent company of LawPay, MyCase, CasePeer and DocketWise. She is the nationally recognized author of Cloud Computing for Lawyers and is a co-author of Social Media for Lawyers: The Next Frontier, both published by the American Bar Association. She writes regular columns for ABAJournal.com and Above the Law, has authored hundreds of articles for other publications, and she regularly speaks at conferences regarding the intersection of law and emerging technologies. Follow her on LinkedIn, or she can be reached at [email protected].

This column reflects the opinions of the author and not necessarily the views of the ABA Journal—or the American Bar Association.

#2023. Attorney disbarred after mishandling over $659K in tragic toddler death

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A prominent Detroit lawyer has been disbarred and was ordered to pay over $659,000 in restitution after handling a case involving the accidental death of a 3-year-old in which the child’s family never received funds from the wrongful death settlement. (Image from Shutterstock)

A prominent Detroit lawyer has been disbarred and was ordered to pay over $659,000 in restitution after handling a case involving the accidental death of a 3-year-old in which the child’s family never received funds from the wrongful death settlement.

Gregory Rohl pleaded no contest to a Michigan ruling of disbarment and restitution for “misappropriating and failing to distribute court-ordered shares.” Rohl was disbarred effective Oct. 1.

He worked on the case of 3-year-old Lilliana, who died after a 350-pound table fell from the wall mount while she was attending the Head Start program at St. Albert the Great Catholic Church in Dearborn Heights, Michigan.

Tabatha Kerr, Lilliana’s mother, is seeking the settlement awarded to her family—including her daughter’s twin sister and a brother—after the 2017 accident.

“I promised my daughter that I would fight until my very last breath, and unfortunately, I’m still here fighting,” Kerr said.

The ruling described Rohl’s conduct as “contrary to justice, ethics, honesty or good morals,” according to coverage on ClickonDetroit.com.

“I have never knowingly taken any monies from any client ever, but the account was in my name, making me ultimately responsible for any loss,” Rohl said in an online statement.

He was ordered to pay $659,821.44 in restitution plus $1,446.14 in other costs.

Rohl, who opened his law office in 1994, worked on the legal team defending Dr. Jack Kevorkian in the 1990s. The attorney claims that his deceased business partner and office manager was partly responsible for the missing funds in Lilliana’s case.

#2016. Cold activates a BH2 metabolic network promoting longevity and proteostasis

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

Moderately cold temperatures extend lifespan and promote health across species1,2,3, yet the molecular mechanisms underlying these benefits remain unclear. Here we define a metabolite-driven, multi-tissue signalling system that links cold temperature to enhanced longevity and proteostasis in Caenorhabditis elegans. Cold temperature upregulates the biopterin-synthesis enzyme CAT-4 (GCH1 in humans), increasing production of dihydrobiopterin (BH2), a transportable reservoir for the bioactive cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4). BH2 is produced in muscle and transported to distal tissues, including the intestine and neurons, where it is converted into BH4 by the salvage enzyme DHFR-1. BH4 functions as an essential cofactor for phenylalanine hydroxylase PAH-1, promoting tyrosine synthesis and extending lifespan. This BH2–BH4 axis also prevents disease-related protein aggregation and subsequent neuronal dysfunction during ageing. Importantly, increasing BH2 synthesis specifically in muscle is sufficient to promote longevity and suppress pathological protein aggregation in distal tissues, even at normal or elevated temperatures. In human cells, BH2 or tyrosine supplementation reduces aggregation of polyQ-expanded proteins associated with Huntington’s disease, as well as mutant variants of TDP-43 and FUS that cause amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. Together, these findings identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite of which the synthesis, intertissue transport and conversion into bioactive BH4 preserve organism-wide proteostasis and extend lifespan.

Emerging evidence demonstrates that moderately cold temperatures delay ageing and promote health across species1. Lowering body temperature extends lifespan in both poikilotherms, including C. elegans, Drosophila melanogaster and various fish species, and homeotherms, including rodents1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8. In adult C. elegans, a shift from standard (20 °C) to moderately low temperature (15 °C) markedly extends lifespan2,9,10,11,12. In rodents, a mild decrease in body temperature by only around 0.5 °C prolongs lifespan, whereas a comparable increase shortens it3,4. Lower body temperature also correlates with longevity in humans13,14. Notably, average human body temperature has declined since the Industrial Revolution, raising the possibility that changes in body temperature may have contributed to increased longevity over recent decades13. Together, these findings suggest that moderate cold engages evolutionarily conserved programs that promote longevity.

Despite being recognized for more than a century15, the mechanisms by which moderate cold temperature promotes longevity remain largely unclear. Historically, cold-induced lifespan extension was interpreted as a consequence of passive thermodynamic effects, including reduced metabolic rate and slower biochemical reaction kinetics. However, studies in C. elegans revealed that cold-induced longevity is an actively regulated process2,9,10,11,12. For example, the temperature-sensitive TRPA-1 ion channel promotes longevity at 15–20 °C but not at warmer temperatures, indicating that low temperature is sensed and transduced into longevity mechanisms11,12. Moreover, cold temperature induces transcriptional programs that enhance proteostasis2,9.

Despite these advances, a fundamental question remains of what systemic signals coordinate cold-induced responses across tissues. Small metabolites are ideally suited for this role because they can act as rapidly diffusible regulators integrating metabolism, enzymatic activity and intertissue signalling. We therefore hypothesized that cold temperature induces metabolite-based signals that coordinate metabolic, proteostatic and longevity pathways across tissues. We further hypothesized that such metabolites could function as temperature-independent effectors capable of recapitulating the beneficial effects of cold temperature even at higher temperatures.

Here we identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite of which the synthesis, intertissue transport and conversion into the bioactive enzymatic cofactor BH4 constitute a multi-tissue regulatory system that promotes longevity and maintains proteostasis across tissues. Through metabolomic, genetic and physiological analyses, we find that elevated BH2 synthesis in muscle and its conversion to BH4 in distal tissues promotes tyrosine synthesis, thereby mediating key aspects of cold-induced longevity. This pathway can be leveraged to confer longevity and proteostasis benefits at normal and elevated temperatures in both C. elegans and human cells.

Exposure of C. elegans to moderately low temperature (15 °C) after development induces a considerable extension of lifespan2,10,11,12. To assess metabolic changes associated with this effect, we raised wild-type worms at the standard temperature (20 °C) until adulthood and subsequently transferred them to 15 °C, 20 °C or 25 °C. Untargeted metabolomics analysis on day 5 of adulthood identified 64 metabolites that were significantly increased at 15 °C relative to both 20 °C and 25 °C (Fig. 1a and Supplementary Table 1). An independent experiment confirmed 45 of these metabolites, with strong concordance between datasets (Extended Data Fig. 1a–d and Supplementary Table 2).

a, Metabolomics of day 5 adult wild-type worms at 15 °C versus 20 °C (left) or 25 °C (right). n = 5 biological replicates. The volcano plots show log2-transformed fold change (log2[FC]) versus −log10-transformed FDR-adjusted P value (−log10[q]). The red dots indicate the 45 cold-induced metabolites increased at 15 °C versus both 20 °C and 25 °C in two independent experiments (FDR-adjusted P < 0.01; Supplementary Tables 1 and 2). b, Schematic of de novo, salvage and recycling BH4 synthesis pathways. Cold-induced metabolites are shown in red font; key enzymes required for cold-induced longevity are outlined by red boxes. C. elegans lacks sepiapterin reductase (SPR). The diagram was created using BioRender; Vilchez, D. https://BioRender.com/mno2d7j (2026). c, BH2 levels in adult wild-type worms across ages at 20 °C or 15 °C. The red dots indicate proteins commonly increased at 15 °C versus 20 °C and 25 °C (FDR < 0.05). f, BH4 levels in day 5 adult sterile fer-15;fem-1 worms treated after development with vector or cat-4 RNAi at 15 °C or 20 °C. Data are mean ± s.e.m., relative to 20 °C + vector RNAi. n = 8 independent experiments. g, cat-4 RNAi after development shortens the lifespan of wild-type worms at 15 °C (P < 0.0001) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.2595) or 25 °C (P = 0.8993). h, dhfr-1 RNAi after development shortens lifespan at 15 °C (P = 0.0005) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.7861). For g,h, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of two independent experiments. Statistical analysis was performed using two-sided t-tests with FDR correction (a,e), two-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test (c,d) or Fisher’s least significant difference (LSD) test (f), and two-sided log-rank tests (g,h).

Among the 45 reproducibly cold-induced metabolites, several were linked to BH4 synthesis and its function as an enzymatic cofactor, including GTP, BH2, tryptophan, phenylalanine and tyrosine (Fig. 1a,b). As BH2 was among the most strongly induced metabolites at cold temperature (Fig. 1a), we focused subsequent analyses on this metabolite. Cold exposure after development increased BH2 levels from day 1 of adulthood, and BH2 remained elevated at 15 °C relative to age-matched animals at 20 °C until at least day 10 (Fig. 1c and Supplementary Table 3). BH2 induction was not a general consequence of longevity, as reduced insulin signalling, a distinct longevity pathway, did not increase BH2 levels at 20 °C (Extended Data Fig. 1e and Supplementary Table 4).

Loss of the temperature-sensitive trpa-1 channel shortens lifespan under both standard and cold temperatures11. However, worms still exhibit substantially extended lifespan at 15 °C compared with at 20 °C, indicating that cold-induced longevity is not solely mediated by TRPA-1-dependent pathways. Loss of trpa-1 did not reduce BH2 levels at cold temperature (Fig. 1d and Supplementary Table 1), demonstrating that elevated BH2 synthesis is induced independently of canonical TRPA-1 temperature sensing.

We next examined whether BH2 reflects a regulated pathway rather than a passive metabolic consequence of reduced temperature. Quantitative proteomics identified GTP cyclohydrolase I (GCH1 in humans, CAT-4 in C. elegans), the first and rate-limiting enzyme in BH2 synthesis16,17, as one of the most strongly induced proteins at 15 °C (Fig. 1e and Supplementary Table 5). Cold temperature also increased cat-4 mRNA amounts, indicating that CAT-4 induction occurs, at least in part, at the transcriptional level (Extended Data Fig. 2a). MED15 (human)/MDT-15 (C. elegans), a key transcriptional regulator of metabolic responses, is required for cold-induced longevity9. Importantly, knockdown of mdt-15 reduced cat-4 transcript levels at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2b), indicating that cold-induced CAT-4 expression is part of a regulated transcriptional program.

Although BH2 is an inactive metabolite, it functions as a key intermediate in the synthesis and intertissue transport of its reduced, bioactive form, BH416,17. As BH4 is chemically unstable and readily oxidized, the more-stable BH2 form serves as a major organismal reservoir of this bioactive metabolite16,17. Consequently, cat-4-mutant worms are severely depleted of BH418. Consistent with the essential role of BH4 as a cofactor for multiple enzymes, these worms exhibit increased fragility compared with wild-type worms18. Accordingly, cat-4-mutant worms were short lived under both cold and standard temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 2c–e). Knockdown of cat-4 from early development also shortened lifespan at both 15 °C and 20 °C, although the effect was more pronounced under cold temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 2f).

As cold temperature during adulthood was sufficient to induce CAT-4 expression, BH2 synthesis and lifespan extension, we next examined the effects of cat-4 knockdown after development. As BH4 could not be detected by metabolomics because of its chemical instability, we quantified BH4 using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Cold temperature induced a significant but modest increase in BH4 levels (Fig. 1f), consistent with BH2 serving as the major intermediate reservoir for this metabolite16,17. Knockdown of cat-4 after development decreased BH4 levels under both standard and cold temperatures, although this reduction was moderate (Fig. 1f). Importantly, cat-4 RNA interference (RNAi) decreased BH4 at cold temperature only to levels comparable to those observed at standard temperature without knockdown (Fig. 1f). Thus, BH4 levels remained within a physiological range, enabling us to determine whether elevated CAT-4 activity during adulthood is required for cold-induced longevity. Notably, knockdown of cat-4 after development diminished the lifespan extension induced by cold temperature, but had no significant effect on lifespan at 20 °C or 25 °C (Fig. 1g).

To further dissect the adult-specific requirement for elevated CAT-4 in cold-induced longevity, we generated an auxin-inducible degron (AID) strain enabling conditional degradation of CAT-4 protein in somatic tissues (Extended Data Fig. 2g). CAT-4 degradation from early development substantially reduced lifespan at 20 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2h). By contrast, degradation of CAT-4 after development did not affect lifespan at 20 °C, but specifically shortened longevity at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2i). These results further indicate that CAT-4 activity is essential during development for adult viability, whereas increased CAT-4 activity during adulthood specifically contributes to cold-induced longevity.

Prompted by these findings, we next examined whether CAT-4 activity also contributes to lifespan extension in other longevity paradigms. Knockdown of cat-4 in mutant worms with reduced insulin signalling did not affect their extended lifespan at standard temperature, but only shortened lifespan at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b). By contrast, knockdown of cat-4 shortened lifespan in long-lived worms with reduced mitochondrial electron-transport chain activity or reduced food intake at both cold and standard temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 3c–h), suggesting that CAT-4 activity contributes to other longevity paradigms beyond cold temperature.

Downstream of activity of GCH1/CAT-4, bioactive BH4 can be generated through three pathways: the de novo, salvage and recycling pathways16,17 (Fig. 1b). In the de novo pathway, a GCH1/CAT-4-dependent intermediate is converted to BH4 by sepiapterin reductase. In the salvage pathway, this intermediate is instead converted to BH2 by either aldose reductase (AKR1B1 (human), Y39G8B.1 (C. elegans)) or carbonyl reductase 1 (CBR1 (human), DHS-31 (C. elegans))16,19. This pathway operates alongside de novo BH4 synthesis, but is particularly important in mammalian tissues with low sepiapterin reductase activity and in organisms such as C. elegans, which lack a sepiapterin reductase orthologue16,18. Knockdown of the BH2-producing enzyme Y39G8B.1 reduced lifespan specifically at 15 °C, whereas loss of dhs-31 produced a smaller, non-significant effect (Extended Data Fig. 4a). In the salvage pathway, BH2 is further converted into bioactive BH4 by dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR (human), DHFR-1 (C. elegans)). Importantly, knockdown of dhfr-1 after development decreased longevity at cold temperature but did not shorten lifespan at standard temperature (Fig. 1h). These results indicate that, downstream of CAT-4 activity, the conversion of BH2 into bioactive BH4 by DHFR-1 contributes to cold-induced longevity.

A recycling mechanism regenerates BH4 after its use as an enzymatic cofactor16,18 (Fig. 1b). In C. elegans, the recycling pathway is not required to maintain steady-state BH4 levels and becomes rate-limiting only under conditions of low BH4 synthesis, such as in reduction-of-function cat-4 mutants18. Subsequently, knockdown of recycling enzymes did not shorten the lifespan of wild-type worms at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 4b). Likewise, mutant worms defective in BH4 recycling were not short lived at 15 °C compared with wild-type worms (Extended Data Fig. 4c,d), indicating that this pathway is dispensable for cold-induced longevity. Together, our findings support a central role of CAT-4 activity and the salvage pathway in elevated BH4 synthesis for cold-induced longevity.

To determine the tissues in which CAT-4 is expressed, we generated a GFP-based fluorescent reporter driven by the endogenous cat-4 promoter. Knockdown of cat-4 reduced the GFP signal, validating the reporter (Extended Data Fig. 5a). At standard temperature, CAT-4 was mostly expressed in the anterior intestine, and cold exposure further increased CAT-4 levels in this region (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b). In contrast to the intestine, CAT-4 was barely detectable in muscle at 20 °C (Fig. 2a). However, cold temperature triggered robust expression of cat-4 in the muscle, an effect that was abolished by cat-4 RNAi treatment (Fig. 2a,b). To assess whether elevated BH2 synthesis in these tissues promotes organismal longevity at cold temperature, we performed tissue-specific knockdown experiments. Notably, knockdown of cat-4 in the muscle alone reduced cold-induced longevity, whereas knockdown in the intestine had no effect on lifespan (Fig. 2c,d). Likewise, knockdown of cat-4 in neurons, epidermis or germline did not shorten lifespan at 15 °C (Fig. 2e–g).

a, Representative images of day 5 adult worms expressing cat-4p::cat-4::SL2::GFP at the indicated temperatures. Scale bars, 20 µm (a,b). Images are representative of three independent experiments. c–g, Tissue-specific cat-4 knockdown (KD) after development. Muscle-specific knockdown shortens cold-induced longevity (c; P < 0.0001), whereas intestinal (d; P = 0.5374), neuronal (e; P = 0.8708), epidermal (f; P = 0.8339) or germline (g; P = 0.2604) knockdown does not. h, Single knockdown of ent-1 (P < 0.0001), ent-5 (P < 0.0001) or ent-6 (P < 0.0001), but not ent-4 (P = 0.9091), shortens lifespan at 15 °C. i, Knockdown of ent-1 (P = 0.2209), ent-5 (P = 0.9840) or ent-6 (P = 0.1278) does not shorten lifespan at 20 °C. j–n, Tissue-specific dhfr-1 RNAi after development. Muscle (j; P = 0.1076) or epidermal (k; P = 0.1830) knockdown does not affect cold-induced longevity, whereas neuronal (l; P < 0.0001), intestinal (m; P = 0.0001) or germline (n; P = 0.0001) knockdown reduces lifespan at 15 °C. In c–n, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of at least two independent experiments. Statistical comparisons were performed using two-sided log-rank tests. Data are mean ± s.e.m. Details of statistical analyses, and replicate data of lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7.

To further investigate the link between cold-induced CAT-4 expression in muscle and longevity, we generated worms in which auxin-inducible degradation of CAT-4 was restricted to muscle. We confirmed that auxin treatment specifically depleted CAT-4 in the muscle of these animals, whereas ubiquitous somatic degradation reduced CAT-4 levels in both muscle and intestine (Extended Data Fig. 5c,d). Notably, muscle-specific degradation of CAT-4 after development was sufficient to fully phenocopy the longevity-shortening effects of ubiquitous somatic CAT-4 degradation at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 5e). This effect was specific to cold temperature, as muscle-specific CAT-4 degradation did not affect lifespan at standard temperature (Extended Data Fig. 5f). Together, these results identify muscle as a critical tissue in which elevated BH2 synthesis promotes cold-induced longevity.

BH4 is essential for the function of amino acid hydroxylases (TPH1 (human), TPH-1 (C. elegans); TH (human), CAT-2 (C. elegans); PAH (human), PAH-1 (C. elegans) and alkylglycerol monooxygenase (AGMO (human), AGMO-1 (C. elegans)). However, these enzymes are not expressed in muscle cells18, raising the possibility that BH4 acts in a cell non-autonomous manner. BH2 is the principal transport intermediate for BH4 distribution between tissues, enabling the rapid generation and utilization of bioactive BH4 in distal tissues16,17. We therefore hypothesized that cold temperature increases BH2 synthesis in muscle cells, which is then released and transported to distal tissues, where it is converted into BH4 to activate its target enzymes. In humans, the uptake of BH2 is mediated by the transmembrane nucleoside transporters ENT1 (SLC29A1) and ENT2 (SLC29A2)16. C. elegans expresses seven orthologues of human ENT1 and ENT2 (ent-1 to ent-7). We found that single knockdown of different orthologues, such as ent-1, ent-5 and ent-6, specifically shortens lifespan at 15 °C but not 20 °C (Fig. 2h,i).

Once BH2 is taken up by distal cells, it can be converted into bioactive BH4 through the salvage pathway by the DHFR-1 enzyme16,20. Consequently, BH4 redistribution efficiency depends on DHFR-1 activity, which regulates local BH4 availability. While BH2 synthesis through CAT-4 activity in muscle cells is required for cold-induced longevity (Fig. 2c), we found that muscle-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 does not shorten lifespan at 15 °C (Fig. 2j). Similarly, dhfr-1 knockdown in the epidermis had no effect on longevity at 15 °C (Fig. 2k). By contrast, tissue-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 in neurons, intestine and germline reduced cold-induced longevity (Fig. 2l–n). Moreover, neither neuron-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 nor muscle-specific knockdown of cat-4 further reduced the shortened lifespan of ent-1 mutant animals at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 5g,h). These results support the notion that cold temperature induces BH2 synthesis in muscle cells, after which, BH2 is released and converted to bioactive BH4 in distal tissues such as neurons, intestine and germline, where it promotes cold-induced longevity.

To further examine the cell non-autonomous effects of BH2, we focused on the intestine, a tissue that progressively loses structural integrity during ageing21,22. To this end, we assessed the intermediate filament IFB-2 because this protein is expressed exclusively in the intestine and undergoes age-related changes that can be readily monitored22. In young animals, IFB-2 is confined to the apical cytoplasm of intestinal cells. With age, IFB-2 levels increase and the protein becomes mislocalized throughout the cytoplasm, where it forms aggregates that contribute to intestinal decline22. We found that cold temperature prevents IFB-2 mislocalization and aggregation during ageing (Extended Data Fig. 6a). However, loss of cat-4 abolished these protective effects (Extended Data Fig. 6a). Notably, muscle-specific knockdown of cat-4 was sufficient to block the cold-induced suppression of intestinal IFB-2 aggregation (Extended Data Fig. 6b). Collectively, these findings indicate that cold-induced BH2 synthesis in muscle exerts cell non-autonomous effects, such as preserving intestinal integrity during ageing.

BH2 supplementation is often more effective at increasing BH4 levels than direct BH4 administration16,20. However, C. elegans exhibits a limited capacity for biopterin uptake, including BH2, and exogenous supplementation results in only a modest increase in biopterin levels18. Despite this limitation, BH2 supplementation in adult worms led to a slight but significant extension of cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 7a). Although BH2 supplementation did not extend lifespan at standard temperature, it delayed the age-related decline in motility, a key indicator of healthspan (Extended Data Fig. 7b,c). Although BH2-treated worms did not display overt signs of sickness, we further assessed the possibility that these beneficial effects arise from a hormetic stress response, such as that induced by mitochondrial dysfunction23,24. However, BH2 supplementation did not reduce brood size and did not induce the mitochondrial unfolded protein response (Extended Data Fig. 7d,e), two commonly used indicators of physiological stress.

Given the limited uptake of exogenous biopterins in worms, we used a genetic approach to assess the full potential of these metabolites to extend longevity. Ubiquitous overexpression of cat-4 in somatic tissues resulted in a substantial increase in cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 7f). Notably, overexpression of cat-4 in the muscle alone was sufficient to increase organismal levels of bioactive BH4 and extend lifespan not only at 15 °C, but also at 20 °C and 25 °C (Fig. 3a,b and Extended Data Fig. 7g–i). Moreover, muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression also extended lifespan in loss-of-function trpa-1 mutant worms across different temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 7j–l). These findings indicate that increasing CAT-4 activity in muscle promotes longevity independently of both temperature and the cold-sensitive channel TRPA-1.
